# Decision register

Mirrors sheet 09 of the Database Specification. **Do not change a settled decision without raising it explicitly first.**

## Settled

| Ref | Date | Area | Decision | Why |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| D-01 | 07 Sep 2026 | Architecture | Build Labourmax-HR greenfield and fully independent of Primax HR — no shared code, no shared database | Keeps the two products separately brandable, deployable and priced. Cost is re-solving PAYE, UIF, SDL and multi-tenancy from scratch. |
| D-02 | 07 Sep 2026 | Scope | Self-registration, subscription and payment gateway are fully in scope for v1 | The product is sold self-service with no implementation consultant, so registration through to first payroll must succeed with no human at Labourmax involved. |
| D-03 | 07 Sep 2026 | Specification | The table specification is produced at full DDL level | Sheet 02 is buildable directly into Django models and migrations rather than being a discussion document. |
| D-04 | 10 Sep 2026 | Tenancy | One person may hold memberships in several tenants | Supports a bookkeeper or agent serving multiple households. Login gains a tenant picker; app_user.last_tenant_id remembers where they were. |
| D-05 | 10 Sep 2026 | Tenancy | Ownership transfer is an explicit two-sided action requiring re-authentication from both parties | New table tenant_ownership_transfer. The outgoing owner initiates, the incoming admin accepts, the request lapses in 72 hours. A one-click transfer would be a one-click account takeover. |
| D-06 | 10 Sep 2026 | Tenancy | Revoking a membership must not prevent a later membership for the same or a different person in that role | SCHEMA CHANGE: UNIQUE (tenant_id, user_id) becomes a partial unique WHERE revoked_at IS NULL. As originally specified, a revoked user could never be re-added. |
| D-07 | 10 Sep 2026 | Leave | An admin who is also an employee may not approve their own leave; it escalates one level to the owner | Where the owner is themselves the applicant and no other approver exists, they may approve with a mandatory recorded reason, flagged on the record and visible in the leave register. |
| D-08 | 10 Sep 2026 | Access | A terminated employee keeps read access for two months, then it lapses regardless | tenant_membership.access_expires_at set to termination date + 2 months. Payslips must be downloaded inside that window. |
| D-09 | 10 Sep 2026 | Statutory output | The IRP5 is emailed on issue rather than held behind a login | An IRP5 is only available after the tax year closes, which can be more than a year after termination — long after the two-month window shuts. Requires bounce handling that surfaces to the employer. |
| D-10 | 10 Sep 2026 | Identity | Both an email address and a verified mobile number are mandatory, for employee logins as well as employer users | app_user.mobile_number becomes NOT NULL with mobile_verified_at. Safety valve: employee.has_no_email is an honest flag that routes delivery to SMS, so employers do not invent addresses to satisfy a mandatory field. |
| D-11 | 10 Sep 2026 | Security | Payslips are delivered as a secure link, not as an ID-number-protected PDF attachment | An SA ID number has very little entropy — date of birth is widely known, the last digit is a checksum, leaving roughly 20,000 combinations for a known person. It is also the single item POPIA most wants protected. New table secure_document_link; the employee authenticates with a mobile OTP at the portal. |
| D-12 | 10 Sep 2026 | Security | Secure links expire — 90 days for a payslip, 12 months for an IRP5 — and are re-issuable by the employer | A lapsed link must be a self-service re-send, not a support call. |
| D-13 | 10 Sep 2026 | Cost | A successful OTP trusts the device for 30 days | Without it, 200 employees viewing twelve payslips a year is 2,400 SMSes per client per year. With it, roughly one OTP per employee per month. |
| D-14 | 10 Sep 2026 | Compliance | Printing and handing over a payslip remains a first-class delivery channel | BCEA s33 requires the employer to give the employee written particulars of payment. A link satisfies that for a smartphone user and nothing at all for a worker with a feature phone and no data. |
| D-15 | 10 Sep 2026 | Employee list | The employee list groups by pay group rather than by pay basis | A pay group is what a payroll run actually targets, and it carries the basis plus the calendar. Two weekly employees on different week-ending days belong in different runs. |
| D-16 | 10 Sep 2026 | Employee list | Default sort is by first name for domestic employers and by surname for contract cleaning employers, overridable and remembered | Seeded into employer_setting from the sector at onboarding. The same setting drives the attendance grid and the payroll run order — one order everywhere, or the muscle memory is lost. |
| D-17 | 10 Sep 2026 | Employee list | Locale-aware ICU collation on the sort columns, set in the creating migration | Deterministic ICU so LIKE and index-backed search still work; lowercasing handles case. Changing a collation later rewrites the table and every index on it. |
| D-18 | 10 Sep 2026 | Employee list | Denormalised current-state cache on employee: current_pay_group_id and current_pay_basis | Pay basis lives on an effective-dated table, so grouping by it would join and date-filter on every render. Maintained on write AND by a nightly job, because a future-dated change must flip on its effective date with nobody touching the record. |
| D-19 | 10 Sep 2026 | UX | Group headers are suppressed below four employees | Group headings over a two-row list are clutter, and that is the domestic employer's entire experience of the product. |
| D-20 | 12 Sep 2026 | Attendance | The monthly capture grid pre-fills every cell from the employee's work schedule; the employer overrides only the exceptions | This is what makes capture fast. Single-letter codes for day types, keyboard navigation, live bucketing into ordinary, overtime, Sunday and public holiday hours, and a live exception panel that gates approval. Prototype built and reviewed 12 September. |
| D-21 | 12 Sep 2026 | Attendance | Blank and zero are different facts | Blank means nobody has told us and blocks the payroll run; zero means they did not work and does not. Rendered distinctly in the grid. |
| D-22 | 12 Sep 2026 | Attendance | A public holiday not worked and a public holiday worked are separate codes | A public holiday falling on an ordinary working day is paid whether or not it is worked; worked, it attracts double. Two payslip lines from one column, and confusing them is a common way to underpay. |
| D-23 | 07 Sep 2026 | Scope | Employee self-service ships in v1 | Was open at v1.0. Now settled by consequence: the brief requires online leave application by employees, and secure-link payslip delivery requires the portal. |
| D-24 | 12 Sep 2026 | Database | PostgreSQL is the database. MySQL is no longer under consideration | Row-level security as a second isolation wall, exclusion constraints on effective-dated ranges, native range partitioning for the audit and attendance tables, and indexed JSONB for calculation traces. Closes at the first migration: the MySQL equivalents in the column dictionary become reference only. |
| D-25 | 12 Sep 2026 | Attendance | The capture grid does not pre-fill for attendance-driven pay bases — hourly and daily. Salaried bases (weekly, fortnightly, monthly) still pre-fill | An hourly or daily worker is paid strictly off what was captured, so an employer must not be able to approve a month they never looked at. Their days open blank and render as uncaptured, which blocks the payroll run until filled. The dividing line is how the base is paid, not which base it is: if pay follows attendance, the grid never assumes it. Bulk fill from schedule and copy-last-week remain available as explicit employer actions — the difference is that a person chooses them. |
| D-26 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Payment details are captured and verified before the trial starts, under a service agreement created at signup | Framed as a free trial with a 7-day cancellation window inside the agreement, rather than a trial that converts into a contract on day 8. The contract exists from day one, which is cleaner to enforce and easier to defend. Closes O-05. |
| D-27 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Trial dates: cancel-by is signup + 7 days to 23:59:59 SAST, first charge is signup + 8 days | Signup on the 12th means cancel by the 19th and charge on the 20th - deliberately one day more lenient than advertised. Both dates are computed once at acceptance, stored on tenant_service_agreement, and rendered from those stored values on every surface. Nothing recalculates a date. |
| D-28 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | The trial-end charge job runs at 09:00 SAST, re-reads subscription status immediately before calling the gateway, and is idempotent on tenant plus charge date | A midnight batch that trusts a status read at queue time is how someone gets charged after cancelling at 23:58. A guard clause in the job itself refuses to charge before first_charge_date. Same-day retries at 13:00 and 17:00, then the dunning ladder. |
| D-29 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | The recurring cycle anchors to the lesser of the first-charge day and 28 | A first charge on the 30th means every subsequent month bills on the 28th. Month-end clamping logic would make February behave differently from March. |
| D-30 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | The signup disclosure sentence carries the actual selected amount and both exact dates, in plain text immediately above the button | Not in the terms, not a tooltip, not behind a link. Charge terms first, cancellation last. The amount is the real figure for the plan and band selected, so the plan must be chosen before the sentence renders. |
| D-31 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Unpaid accounts are suspended until payment is received, with a payment prompt on login | Suspended means blocked from working - no payroll runs, no employee changes - while existing payslips and IRP5s stay viewable and downloadable, because the employer carries a five-year retention obligation on them. A failed first charge at trial end blocks immediately; a failed renewal for an established customer goes through the dunning ladder first. |
| D-32 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Cancelling during the trial deactivates the payment method and incurs no charge or liability | The gateway token is deleted. Data is kept 30 days with an export offered, then purged - POPIA does not permit holding employee personal information for someone who never became a customer. |
| D-33 | 12 Sep 2026 | Pricing | Domestic standard plan includes 3 active employees, then charges per head | Active means not terminated and not archived. Terminated employees are retained for five years and cost storage, but are not billable. |
| D-34 | 12 Sep 2026 | Pricing | Contract cleaning is banded, and every band above the entry band is a flat base plus a per-head charge for employees above its threshold | Reading A. Charge = base + (headcount - threshold) x per-head rate. This removes the cliff at each boundary: an employer going from 5 cleaners to 6 pays for one extra person, not a whole band's difference. New table plan_price_band; bands are data, so repricing needs no release. |
| D-35 | 12 Sep 2026 | Pricing | Bands are 1-5, 6-10, 11-30, 31-50, 51-100, and over 100 which routes to contact sales | Above a certain size an employer wants a conversation before handing over a card. The top band is flagged requires_contact_sales rather than offering self-service checkout. |
| D-36 | 12 Sep 2026 | Pricing | Contract cleaning signup asks 'How many cleaners do you employ?' as a dropdown that selects the band and prices the disclosure sentence, and pre-fills nothing else | It does not seed employee records, pay groups or anything else. The declared band is stored so the disclosure can be reconstructed, and later compared against actual headcount to see how accurate declarations are. |
| D-37 | 12 Sep 2026 | Pricing | The day-8 charge is the lesser of the disclosed amount and the amount for actual headcount, and may never exceed what the signup sentence said | Understating gets the employer one cheap month, which costs little and removes every argument. Overstating means they are charged the lower actual figure. From the second invoice, actual active headcount governs. |
| D-38 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Bill on active headcount at invoice generation, but record four usage metrics alongside it | Active at invoice, distinct employees paid, payslips finalised, and the high-water mark of active employees during the period. Payroll processed for someone terminated mid-period is real leakage in high-churn contract cleaning, and the high-water mark exposes add-and-remove behaviour around invoice dates without changing the billing rule. Informational at launch; revisit after six months of data. |
| D-39 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Adding an employee beyond the current band prompts at that moment with the new price and an inline adjustment option | Names both prices, states that nothing is charged today, and gives the real next-invoice date. Recorded as a subscription_event with the acknowledging user, which is the evidence if the higher invoice is ever queried. A bulk import evaluates the whole batch and prompts once, after preview. |
| D-40 | 12 Sep 2026 | Billing | Band downgrades apply automatically at the next invoice with a notification, and never prompt | Deliberately asymmetric. An upgrade costs money and needs consent; nobody objects to a smaller bill, so asking for it is friction with no purpose. |
| D-41 | 12 Sep 2026 | Documents | One document table with an exclusive arc, attaching to a tenant, employer, employee or workplace, replacing the earlier employee_document | SCHEMA CHANGE: employee_document is removed; document and document_category replace it. Four nullable owner columns with a CHECK that exactly one is set gives real referential integrity, unlike a generic owner_type/owner_id pair which produces orphans the first time a record is deleted. One table means one upload path, one virus scan, one expiry job, one retention purge and one permission model. |
| D-42 | 12 Sep 2026 | Documents | Workplaces are a document attachment point, so client contracts file against the site | Employees are allocated to a site through employee_position, which makes cost-per-contract reporting possible. Without this, client contracts land on the employer record with the site name typed into the title. |
| D-43 | 12 Sep 2026 | Documents | Document categories are a table, not an enum, carrying the filing rules | What a category attaches to, whether it expires, whether it is required for onboarding, whether the employee may see it, and its default retention. Tenants can add their own without a release. |
| D-44 | 12 Sep 2026 | Documents | Employee self-service shows only the employment contract, payslips and the IRP5 - a whitelist, default deny | document_category.visible_to_employee defaults FALSE and is TRUE for one category. A blacklist would mean a forgotten tick puts a disciplinary letter in the employee's portal. Payslips and IRP5s are not in this table at all - they are reached by secure link. |
| D-45 | 12 Sep 2026 | Documents | CVs are stored for hired employees only; applicant tracking is out of scope | Storing CVs of people who applied and were not hired means holding personal information about people with no employment relationship, with its own consent basis and retention clock. That is a recruitment module, not this one. |
| D-46 | 12 Sep 2026 | Documents | Per-plan storage quota, 20MB upload cap, server-side image downscaling, and an audit row on every download | 500 cleaning tenants at 200 employees and six documents each is over a terabyte. storage.max_mb is a plan_feature limit with a warning at 80%. Every download is audited because these are ID copies and bank letters. |
| D-47 | 12 Sep 2026 | Attendance | Site assignment is a single-site or multi-site option per employee, with no intra-day splitting | Single-site hides the site column from the attendance grid; multi-site exposes a per-day picker. A day belongs to one site. Deliberately small - an allocation table for split days was considered and rejected as disproportionate. |
| D-48 | 12 Sep 2026 | Reporting | Cost per contract reports only what the database already carries. No contract value is stored | Wages, premiums, leave pay, employer UIF and SDL, and the annual bonus accrual shown as its own line because it is a provision rather than a payment. The employer compares against their own client invoice; Labourmax never holds the revenue side. |
| D-49 | 12 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | Statutory rate changes are not ingested automatically. The load is human and verified; the application by effective date is automatic | There is no machine-readable source - SARS and the gazettes publish PDFs. Interpretation is required, as with the 2026 contract cleaning gazette pointing KwaZulu-Natal at a bargaining council agreement rather than giving a number. A wrong rate applied silently across every tenant means reversal runs and corrected IRP5s. |
| D-50 | 12 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | A staleness guard blocks payroll runs beyond the date statutory data is confirmed correct for | reference_data_version.data_current_through. A run whose period ends past it raises a blocking validation issue rather than falling back to superseded rates. The system does not pretend to know something it does not. |
| D-51 | 12 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | The maintenance calendar is held as data in statutory_watch_item, and five recurring reminders were set up outside the product | 15 January season opens, 20 February main load window, 26 February final gate before 1 March, 15 April BCEA threshold, and a quarterly sweep for the irregular items. Each reminder searches the current published figures and reports them against what the system holds, rather than simply prompting. A 'no change' result still advances last_confirmed_date. |
| D-52 | 12 Sep 2026 | Tenancy | A stored file always belongs to exactly one tenant: file_object.tenant_id becomes NOT NULL and the model inherits TenantScopedModel | Found during the P0 migration review: file_object held CVs, ID documents and CIPC documents with a nullable tenant_id, no scoped manager and no RLS policy, and the isolation suite did not discover it. Platform-owned assets (sector document templates, announcement attachments) get their own table in P9 rather than a nullable hole in the table holding customer identity documents. |
| D-53 | 12 Sep 2026 | Tenancy | Security and operations tables with a legitimately nullable tenant_id use a second explicit base, TenantOptionalModel, rather than no protection at all | Applies to otp_challenge, login_audit, audit_log and background_job - an OTP issued before a tenant is picked, a login attempt that failed before we knew who it was. Rules: a tenant session sees only its own rows and never the NULL-tenant ones; a session with no tenant pinned sees the NULL-tenant rows only; the platform console sees everything and only inside platform_context(). AMENDED same day: the third rule was originally 'writable but not readable' for audit_log and background_job. That is not implementable - Django appends RETURNING id to every INSERT and PostgreSQL applies the policy's USING clause to rows returned that way, so a row the USING clause rejects cannot be inserted at all. Given up: a secondary defence against an unauthenticated request reading platform audit rows. Kept: no tenant can ever see another tenant's rows, or the platform's. |
| D-54 | 12 Sep 2026 | Security | Cross-tenant visibility is granted by one named function, platform_context(), which lifts the manager filter and the RLS policy together and logs every entry | The alternative was a flag threaded through call sites, which is unreviewable. One deliberate, greppable, logged hole beats five silent ones. A request never gets platform access - only the superuser console and named maintenance jobs enter it. |
| D-55 | 12 Sep 2026 | Data integrity | Append-only tables are enforced by a database trigger, not by REVOKE UPDATE, DELETE FROM PUBLIC | The revoke does nothing: a table's owner holds its privileges implicitly and the application connects as the owner. Identical trap to row-level security without FORCE - the protection reads convincingly and is absent. The POPIA retention purge (P11) is the one legitimate deleter and sets labourmax.allow_ledger_maintenance for its duration, in the same style as platform_context(). Applied to audit_log at P0; leave_transaction, payslip and payslip_line follow in P6 and P7. |
| D-56 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | Reference data is loaded from a JSON fixture through one loader that refuses the whole file on any problem, and never updates an existing row | All or nothing in one transaction; no citation, no load; a fixture row matching an existing natural key with a different value is refused rather than applied, because a statutory value is superseded by a new effective-dated row and never edited. Foreign keys are natural keys - 'DOMESTIC', never 7 - so a fixture is portable between development, CI and production. The fixture is fingerprinted with SHA-256 onto reference_data_version.checksum, which makes a quietly edited file detectable and an identical re-run a no-op. |
| D-57 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | Closing a superseded period is declared by the fixture, never inferred by the loader | An annual load collides with last year's open-ended row, and the fix is a modification of already-verified data. One top-level key, closes_open_periods_from, states it; every row closed is listed in the load report for the verifier to check against the source document. Without the declaration the exclusion constraint refuses the load, which is the correct outcome. |
| D-58 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | A calculator never queries a statutory table. Every effective-date lookup goes through statutory.resolve, and missing data raises rather than returning None | Written by hand in each calculator the query eventually appears as effective_to__gte, which is off by one day, in one place, and produces a payslip that is wrong only for employees paid on the day a rate changed. Missing data raises because a calculator handed None for the UIF ceiling still produces a payslip. Narrowing falls back in a fixed documented order - grade to area to sector to the National Minimum Wage, which genuinely is the floor - and widening never does. |
| D-59 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | The no-hard-coded-rate rule is a test, not a grep | P2's definition of done ran as a person's grep on the day the phase closed. It now runs on every commit: no Decimal constant outside a very short allowlist and no float literal anywhere in application code, no ORM or clock in calculators/, and no database default on any rule set column. Stated in terms of shape rather than of particular figures, so it keeps working for rates nobody has loaded yet. |
| D-60 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | PAYE bands are stored with income_from set to the 'taxable income above R' figure SARS prints, not the band's printed lower bound | SARS prints band 2 as starting at R245 101. Stored that way, a taxable income of R245 100,50 falls between two bands and resolves to nothing, mid payroll run. Stored as R245 100 the bands touch, and the two readings agree at the touch point by construction - 18% of R245 100 is R44 118, which is band 2's base. Recorded in the notes column of every affected row. |
| D-61 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | SUPERSEDED BY D-118 - WRONG. Contract cleaning areas are A (metros), B (named local councils) and C (KwaZulu-Natal), per the gazette | The model's own help text had B and C the other way round, with KwaZulu-Natal as Area B. GN R.7083 GG 54075 of 3 February 2026 says otherwise. Codes are AREA_A, AREA_B, AREA_C_KZN. Found by reading the gazette rather than the earlier draft - which is the argument for researching primary sources rather than converting the workbook. |
| D-62 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | Verification is gated on the data reconciling with itself, checked by machine before a person signs it off | statutory/checks.py: every PAYE base must equal the tax on the bands below it, every tax threshold must equal its cumulative rebates divided by the lowest rate, bands must touch with no gap, and no citation may be a placeholder like 'TBC'. Published statutory tables are internally redundant, so one mistyped digit stops the ladder reconciling. manage.py checkstatutory runs it; verifystatutory refuses while anything blocking is outstanding. It proves the figures agree with each other, never that they are the right figures - that is the golden tests' job in P7. |
| D-63 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Where a figure cannot be found in a primary source it is absent from the fixture, not estimated | Contract cleaning Area C carries no rate: the gazette gives KwaZulu-Natal no figure and points at the BCCCI collective agreement. Rather than interpolate, the area is loaded with uses_bargaining_council_rates set, the watch list carries it as change_published_not_loaded, and a KwaZulu-Natal contract cleaning employer cannot be onboarded until the agreement is in hand. Two further figures - the UIF ceiling's gazette number and the COIDA earnings ceiling - are loaded from secondary sources and carry a VERIFY note in their notes column. |
| D-64 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Parental leave is stored as months plus days, the maternity restriction is stored as two separate columns, and annual_bonus_month is nullable. THE WORKBOOK NEEDS THE SAME THREE CORRECTIONS | Departures from sheet 02, each found by reading the source. (1) Van Wyk v Minister of Employment and Labour [2025] ZACC 20 gives all parents four months and ten days - four calendar months from January is not the same number of days as four from June, so a single day count would bake one interpretation into the reference data. (2) The column was maternity_pre_birth_reserved_days; the statute's hard rule is the other way round - a birth mother may not work for six weeks AFTER the birth unless certified fit, and separately may start up to four weeks before. Two rules, two columns, and the post-birth one is the one with teeth. (3) annual_bonus_month was NOT NULL with a 1-12 CHECK, so the BCEA default and the domestic sector - neither of which has a statutory bonus - would have had to name a month they never pay in. |
| D-65 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | A rule set column with no statutory figure behind it is loaded as zero with a note saying the figure does not exist, never as a plausible number | Four columns: night_allowance_value on both rows (BCEA s17(2) requires an allowance and deliberately sets no amount - the employer does), and the three standby columns plus the accommodation cap on the BCEA row (neither concept is in the Act; both are SD7's). The notes column carries 'ZERO MEANS THERE IS NO STATUTORY FIGURE' in capitals, because a calculator author reading 0.0000 will otherwise pay nothing for night work. |
| D-66 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Contract cleaning gets no rule set until SD1 has been read, and falls back to the BCEA default in the meantime | A contract cleaning rule set that copied the BCEA values under a citation naming SD1 would hide the gap; the fallback shows it. sector.has_statutory_annual_bonus is True for contract cleaning while no rule set carries the bonus terms, and a test asserts that contradiction stays visible until SD1 is loaded. |
| D-67 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Contract cleaning rule sets loaded from Sectoral Determination 1. Three figures are SD1's own and differ from every other sector | (1) Clause 16 gazettes a REAL night allowance - not less than 10 percent of the hourly wage for each hour between 18:00 and 06:00 - so contract cleaning is the one sector where night_allowance_value carries a figure rather than a zero meaning 'no statutory figure'. (2) Clause 3(2) sets the short-day minimum at SIX hours, against four under BCEA s9A and SD7. (3) Clause 3(3) gives the 4,333-week annual bonus in December, pro-rated as full calendar months over twelve on termination. Everything else - leave, sick leave, family responsibility, severance - matches the BCEA. Supersedes D-66. |
| D-68 | 13 Sep 2026, CLOSED 14 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | CLOSED. `termination_rule_set`'s three `notice_weeks_*` DecimalFields are dropped and replaced by `termination_notice_band`, a cited child table carrying `service_from`/`service_to` and `notice_value`, each with its own unit (days/weeks/months/years for service, days/weeks for notice). BCEA (1/2/4 weeks at 6 months and 1 year) and domestic (1/4 weeks, collapsing to two bands since SD7's 6-months-and-over and over-1-year figures agree) carry over unchanged; SD1 clause 23(1) gets its own two bands for the first time — "during the first four weeks of employment, not less than one working day's [notice]" (23(1)(a)), "four weeks, if the employee has been employed for more than four weeks" (23(1)(b)) | ORIGINALLY: SD1 clause 23 splits at FOUR WEEKS of service, one working day below it and four weeks above, and the three-bucket shape (under-6-months, 6-months-and-over, over-1-year) could express neither the boundary nor the unit — a bare number could not distinguish one working day from a fraction of a week without assuming a five-day working week, which contract cleaning's own six-day shifts make wrong. All three buckets were loaded at four weeks as the safe direction (over-paying the first month rather than under-paying every employee to six months), with the gap recorded in capitals on the row rather than hidden. CLOSED: nothing read the three columns (P7's termination engine does not exist yet), so they are dropped in the same migration that adds their replacement, per D-89's lesson that two sources for one compliance answer is the failure, not a safety net. `tools/build_notice_band_fixture.py` transcribes the carried-over BCEA/domestic figures with the SAME citations the retired columns used, and `statutory/tests/test_notice_bands.py::test_the_shipped_bcea_and_domestic_bands_reproduce_the_retired_columns` is the permanent evidence — asserted against a frozen snapshot of what those columns said, since the columns themselves do not survive this commit to compare against. See D-158 for the boundary-resolution rule this needed, D-159 for the loader mechanism, and O-06's newest entry for what this closure deliberately still leaves open (SD1's pay-in-lieu formulas). |
| D-69 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | sars_source_code becomes a cited table: source_reference (with the not-blank CHECK), source_url and notes. WORKBOOK CHANGE | The code and its wording are SARS's, but the four base flags are not - whether an amount enters the UIF, SDL or COIDA base is a reading of three statutes that each define remuneration or earnings differently. Commission is the standing proof: taxable, inside the SDL leviable amount, outside the UIF base. A boolean with no citation beside it is a compliance decision nobody can audit and nobody dares change. Every loaded row now carries its reasoning, and a test asserts the reasoning is there. |
| D-70 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Nineteen source codes loaded - the ones these two sectors use - rather than the several hundred SARS publishes | Loading the share-scheme and foreign-service codes would mean loading four unreasoned flags apiece into a table whose entire purpose is that somebody has reasoned about them. Deliberately absent: 3901 severance (taxed on the retirement lump sum tables, belongs with P6 termination work), 3616 independent contractors (UIF treatment turns on facts about the person, not the code), and every travel, share and foreign code. |
| D-71 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Two source code invariants are enforced by checkstatutory, whatever the readings are | A non-taxable code cannot sit in the UIF or SDL base, because both are built on the Fourth Schedule definition of remuneration. And a total or a deduction carries no base flags at all, because nothing is ever calculated FROM one - the failure it prevents is UIF charged on the UIF figure. Neither check knows which flags are correct; both catch shapes that are wrong under any reading. |
| D-72 | 13 Sep 2026 | Banking | bank.account_number_min_length and max_length become NULLABLE, and NULL means nobody has confirmed that bank's rule. WORKBOOK CHANGE | Per-bank account number lengths are operational data from the banks and the ACB specification, not one published list; the only figure findable in public is a general nine-to-eleven digit range. The columns were NOT NULL, so loading any bank at all required inventing two numbers for it. A guessed bound is worse than none: too narrow rejects a valid account number, which reads to the employer as a system fault and ends with an employee unpaid on the 25th. Validation falls back to nine-to-eleven when NULL. A second CHECK keeps the two bounds together, because one without the other is a half-loaded rule that looks like a working one. Bank also gains a notes column for the branch code's provenance. |
| D-73 | 13 Sep 2026 | Banking | Twenty-five banks loaded with universal branch codes and no account lengths; defunct and unconfirmed banks are left out entirely | Omitted: VBS Mutual Bank (liquidated), The Royal Bank of Scotland N.V. (exited South Africa), MTN Banking (closed), and Olympus Mobile, People Bank and Permanent Bank, whose current status could not be confirmed. An inactive bank in this table is worse than a missing one - it offers an employer a choice that will bounce the payment. Seven codes rest on a single source and say VERIFY in their notes. A warning-level check flags any branch code that is not six digits, which is the transposition that surfaces as a returned payment. |
| D-74 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | An empty reference database is itself a blocking finding, and checkstatutory now says so instead of printing a green tick | Found in use: Kobus ran checkstatutory against a database where nothing had been loaded and was told 'Every check reconciles'. Every check in the module iterates rows, so over no rows they all return nothing and the command reports success on a system that knows no rates at all - the same shape as the five P0 defects, where protection read convincingly and did nothing because the thing it protected was absent. check_something_is_loaded() is called by the reporting command, NOT by run_all(), because run_all gates verification of a single version and versions are verified in any order - refusing to verify the bank list because wage rates are not loaded yet would be wrong. The success line now names what it counted. |
| D-75 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory maintenance | The fixture load order is data in the codebase, and loadstatutory --all is the way to load the set | Found in use: a shell glob over reference/*.json loads alphabetically, which puts the rule set fixtures before the file that creates the sectors they reference, and the loader correctly refused them. Two of five files silently did not load while the command reported success for the other three. FIXTURE_ORDER lives next to TABLES in statutory/loader.py because it is a property of the data, not of the filenames. A generated test compares it against the directory listing, so a fixture added to reference/ and forgotten fails the suite rather than being quietly skipped - the same pattern as the tenant isolation suite, for the same reason: the failure is silent. --all loads each file as its own version, NOT in one transaction, so a later failure does not roll back an earlier file that loaded cleanly. |
| D-76 | 13 Sep 2026 | Security | FORCE ROW LEVEL SECURITY makes the FOREIGN KEY check itself subject to the policy, so reference tables that tenant tables point at now refuse DELETE by trigger | Found in P3 by a test that expected ProtectedError and got a successful delete plus an orphaned employer row. Deleting a sector from a session with no tenant pinned defeats all three layers: Django's collector queries employer WHERE sector_id = X to find rows to protect and RLS returns none; PostgreSQL's own referential integrity check is subject to the policy because employer has FORCE RLS; and platform_context() does not help because strict tenant tables carry no platform override (D-54). A BEFORE DELETE trigger on the referenced table is the only layer that runs regardless of what the deleting session can see. Applied to sector, sector_area, job_grade, bank, bank_branch and tax_year. Escape hatch labourmax.allow_reference_maintenance, in the same style as the ledger one. |
| D-77 | 13 Sep 2026 | Security | Encrypted columns refuse every lookup but isnull, and carry a _last4 and a keyed _hash companion | Fernet is non-deterministic, so filter(account_number=...) encrypts the search term afresh and matches nothing - an EMPTY RESULT, not an error, which reads as 'no such account' and is how a duplicate check reports that every account is new. EncryptedCharField raises FieldError instead and names the column to use. _hash is a keyed HMAC-SHA256 of the normalised value, not a bare digest: the space of ten-digit account numbers is small enough to enumerate. Both companions are maintained in save(), not by the caller, because a hash somebody forgot to set silently defeats the check it exists for. A missing FIELD_ENCRYPTION_KEY refuses the write rather than falling back to plaintext. |
| D-78 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employers | tenant and employer are separate tables, and employer carries a real FK to statutory.Sector | A tenant is who subscribes; an employer is who employs. A contract cleaning group can run several employing entities under one subscription, each with its own PAYE and COIDA registration, and collapsing the two makes that a schema change rather than a second row. Billing is per tenant, statutory submission is per employer. employer.sector is a foreign key rather than the code string tenant carries, because it is the switch that selects every rule set and minimum wage - a string lets a typo route an employer silently to the BCEA fallback. |
| D-79 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employers | employer_setting is key-value with four typed columns and a CHECK that exactly one holds the value; the registry of what exists lives in code | Settings arrive one at a time over years, and a column per setting means a migration every time somebody wants a new default. The usual key-value cost - everything becomes a string - is paid off the same way statutory_parameter pays it, with typed columns. A setting's existence, type and meaning are code because they are referenced by name in calculators and screens; only the value is data. set_by_employer distinguishes a seeded default from a chosen value, which is what decides whether a changed sector default should reach an existing employer. |
| D-80 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employers | A statutory figure is never an employer setting. The night work allowance is the only genuine exception, and contract cleaning is not even that | BCEA s17(2) requires a night allowance and deliberately names no amount, so for the domestic sector it is the employer's figure and the setting seeds to zero meaning 'nobody has set this'. SD1 clause 16 gazettes 10 percent for contract cleaning, so that sector seeds NIGHT_ALLOWANCE_IS_STATUTORY true and the rule set governs. The overtime multiplier, the leave days and every rate stay in the statutory tables with citations - a setting that shadowed one would be an uncited statutory figure wearing an employer's name. |
| D-81 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employers | A workplace's wage area is DERIVED from its municipality, stored with the date it was resolved, and left NULL when it cannot be resolved | Asking an employer which area they are in gets a confident answer and a wrong one is invisible - payslips that look right and underpay by a couple of rand an hour. Deriving it produces a wrong answer visibly: resolved, or unresolved with a reason a screen can show. Stored rather than computed on read because municipality_area_map is effective-dated and municipal amalgamations have moved workplaces between areas - a 2026 re-run in 2030 must use the 2026 mapping. A CHECK keeps the area and its resolution date together. NOTE: municipality_area_map is empty, so no contract cleaning area resolves today. |
| D-82 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | pay_group carries ONE pay_frequency column with five values, per the workbook. Claude's proposed split of pay_basis from pay_cycle is not adopted | Raised as a design question and settled by reading sheet 02 of Labourmax-HR_Database_Specification_3.xlsx: pay_group.pay_frequency VARCHAR(20) NOT NULL, hourly|daily|weekly|fortnightly|monthly, 'Exactly the five bases in the brief'. The workbook wins (CLAUDE.md). The calendar lives in four further columns - period_end_rule, week_ending_weekday, period_end_day_of_month and payment_day_offset - which is richer than the split would have been anyway. |
| D-83 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | A pay period belongs to the tax year its PAYMENT DATE falls in, not its period end | The workbook says payment_date 'determines which tax year the earnings fall into'. A weekly period ending 27 February that pays on 3 March is earnings in the NEW tax year; assigning by period end moves a week of pay onto the wrong IRP5 and every payslip still looks correct. The generator is written this way and a test asserts the February-to-March boundary specifically. |
| D-84 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | Hourly and daily pay groups take their period cadence from period_end_rule. INTERPRETATION, not from the workbook | 'Hourly' describes how pay is computed, not how often it is paid - an hourly cleaner paid every Friday is on a weekly cadence, and the workbook's single pay_frequency column gives no other way to say so. A week-ending rule gives weekly periods; a month rule gives monthly ones. Recorded here because it is Claude's reading rather than the specification's, and it is the one place P3's calendar goes beyond what sheet 02 states. |
| D-85 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | working_days_in_period does NOT subtract public holidays | A public holiday falling on an ordinary working day is paid, so it remains a day available for pay purposes. Subtracting it would under-count the month for a salaried employee. A five-day week means Monday to Friday, six means Monday to Saturday, seven means every day; any fractional week is pro-rated across calendar days rather than given an invented weekday shape. |
| D-86 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employer setup | pay_group.default_hours_per_day is validated against the sector's working time rule set, not against a constant | The workbook defaults the pattern to 5 days and 9 hours, which coincides with the BCEA ordinary-hours shape - a coincidence, not a statutory column. The limit itself stays in working_time_rule_set with its citation, and clean() reads it through statutory.resolve. Validation rather than a CHECK constraint, because a database constraint would have to hard-code the figure, which is the one thing that must not happen. |
| D-87 | 13 Sep 2026 | Security | A THIRD tenancy base: TenantSharedModel, where a NULL tenant_id means 'available to all' rather than 'belongs to the platform and no tenant may see it' | Sheet 02 annotates payroll_component.tenant_id with 'Null = system component available to all'. TenantOptionalModel means the exact opposite by the same NULL - its whole guarantee is that a tenant NEVER sees the NULL-tenant rows - so reusing it would have made the sixteen system components invisible to every employer, and reusing it with the clause flipped would have opened every platform audit row to every tenant. One policy cannot express both meanings. enable_rls_shared() is the third policy and the first where READ and WRITE differ: a tenant reads the catalogue and its own rows, and writes only its own. The generated isolation suite discovers the new base, asserts no model inherits two bases, and asserts a shared table carries no column naming a person or an amount - because a shared row is readable by every tenant and no policy would report that as a leak. |
| D-88 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | No system payroll component carries a rate. All sixteen are calculation_method='statutory' with default_rate_multiplier and percentage_value NULL | OT_1_5 is a label; the 1.5 is BCEA s10 and lives in working_time_rule_set.overtime_multiplier with a gazette citation and an effective date. A multiplier stored on the component would be a hard-coded statutory rate wearing a data row as a disguise: the March gazette would be loaded, the statutory table updated, and every overtime line would still use the old figure with nothing reporting a problem. Same for both Sunday multipliers - which is also why one column could never hold them, since the rate depends on whether Sunday is an ordinary working day for that employee - the public holiday multiplier, and SD1's 10 percent night allowance. The two rate columns stay, for EMPLOYER-defined components where the number is the employer's own choice. A test fails if any system component carries either. |
| D-89 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | A payroll component's four base flags must equal its SARS source code's, enforced in clean(). Where there is no source code the flags are all false | Sheet 02 puts is_taxable, is_uif_base, is_sdl_base and is_coida_base on payroll_component, and sars_source_code already carries the same four with the reasoning and the citation beside them (D-69). Two copies of a compliance decision is how the copies come to disagree, and the way it surfaces is an EMP201 that does not reconcile to the payslips behind it, months later. The source code is the single source of truth; the component is the copy, and the copy may not differ. The four are separate columns rather than one because the bases genuinely differ - commission is taxable, inside the SDL leviable amount and OUTSIDE the UIF base. |
| D-90 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | SEVERANCE ships INACTIVE with no source code, rather than being pointed at 3601 to make it work | Severance benefits are source code 3901 and are taxed on a SARS directive against the retirement lump sum table, not through the ordinary PAYE tables. 3901 is deliberately not loaded (D-70) and the directive handling does not exist. Pointing the component at 3601 would put a termination payment on the wrong IRP5 line AND tax it at the wrong rate, and every payslip would look correct. The component exists because sheet 02 names it, is_active is false so it cannot reach a payslip, and seedcomponents --activate-severance exists so P6 can turn it on in the same breath as loading 3901. A test asserts the gap, so it cannot close by accident. |
| D-91 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | affects_leave_pay_average follows the BCEA s35(5) determination and is written out as an exact set in a test, with the citation next to it | Government Notice 691 in Government Gazette 24889, in force 1 July 2003. Its rule is a catch-all: ANY cash payment forms part of remuneration for annual leave, notice and severance except those on a short exclusion list - payments enabling work, relocation, gratuities, share schemes, discretionary payments unrelated to hours or performance, entertainment, education. Overtime, Sunday work, public holiday work, night allowances and standby are none of those, so all five are in alongside the basic wage. LEAVE_PAY is false and must stay false or a second period of leave compounds off the first. BONUS_PRO_RATA is false and FLAGGED rather than settled: the catch-all takes in any non-discretionary cash payment and a gazetted bonus is not discretionary, but averaging a once-a-year payment across thirteen weeks overstates a week's remuneration by roughly a third and no source addresses the interaction. Confirm before P5's leave engine ships. |
| D-92 | 13 Sep 2026 | Security | A context block that WRITES outside a request must open a transaction first, and the test for it runs with transaction=True | Found in use, immediately: seedcomponents failed on its first run against a live database with 'new row violates row-level security policy' while all 481 tests passed. set_config(..., true) is TRANSACTION-local - deliberately, so a pooled connection cannot carry one request's tenant into the next. Under autocommit, which is where every management command, Celery task and shell session runs, each statement is its own transaction: platform_context() sets the flag, the next statement commits, and the flag is gone before the INSERT. A request never hits it because ATOMIC_REQUESTS holds one transaction open; a test never hits it because pytest-django wraps each one. So a whole green suite can sit on top of a command that cannot run. The ordering matters too - transaction.atomic() OUTSIDE the context block, because the set_config has to happen inside the transaction it is scoped to. Written up in core/managers.py beside apply_session_variables, which is where the next person will look. |
| D-93 | 13 Sep 2026 | Payroll | A system row is locked by TRIGGER, not by policy, because a DELETE is checked against USING only | An UPDATE is tested against a policy's USING clause and its WITH CHECK clause, so a tenant editing a shared component is refused by the second. A DELETE has no WITH CHECK - there is no new row to check - so a tenant that can legitimately SEE a shared row can delete it, and the catalogue every other employer's payslips point at loses a line. lock_system_rows() refuses UPDATE and DELETE on any row with is_system true, for the same reason no_delete() exists on the reference tables (D-76): a trigger runs regardless of what the deleting session can see, and it binds the table owner, which the application connects as. Two CHECK constraints tie the two concepts together - a shared row must be a system row, and a system row must be a shared row - so there is no unlocked row that everybody can read. The lock binds platform_context() as well: changing a system component's treatment is a migration with a reason attached, because finalised payslip lines point at it. |
| D-94 | 13 Sep 2026 | CI | CI generates its own Fernet key per run, and a Django system check reports a missing one as ONE line rather than as a pile of encryption failures | CI went red on 32541e9, the commit that introduced encryption at rest, and stayed red across four commits while the identical suite was green on Kobus's machine - where setup-windows.ps1 had generated a FIELD_ENCRYPTION_KEY months earlier. The workflow had no key, so every test touching employer_bank_account failed with ImproperlyConfigured from inside core/db/fields.py. The refusal itself is correct and must not change (D-77): an unset key stops the write rather than storing plaintext. What was wrong was the signal. A secret read AT USE - which is the right way to read one, because an empty key must stop a write and not stop the application booting - gives no warning at all until something touches it. core/checks.py registers labourmax.W001, so `manage.py check --fail-level WARNING` (already a CI step, and it runs before the suite) fails with one line naming the setting and the command that generates a key. The key is a LITERAL in the job's env block, not generated per run into $GITHUB_ENV. The generated version worked when tested and was fragile in a way that showed up immediately: it depends on step ordering, so anything that puts a step needing the key ahead of the step producing it fails with a message about the key rather than about the ordering - and the first run after that change failed roughly thirty seconds earlier than the ones before it, at the system check rather than at the suite. A literal in the job env is visible to every step and cannot be got wrong. The usual objection to a committed key is that somebody copies it into a production .env; this one decodes to the ASCII string CI-ONLY-NEVER-USE-IN-PRODUCTION!, so it says what it is when anybody looks, and it protects an ephemeral database destroyed with the runner. |
| D-95 | 13 Sep 2026 | Security | keyed_hash takes a scope, and every multi-tenant hash column passes the tenant. WORKBOOK CHANGE in effect: sheet 02 says 'SHA-256 with per-tenant salt' and this is that, done as HMAC | Without a scope the same ID number produces the SAME digest in every tenant, so anybody holding the column - a support engineer, a read replica, a backup, an analyst joining two tables - can establish that one household's domestic worker is also employed by another subscriber. Nothing decrypts, no policy is bypassed, and no forbidden row is read, which is exactly why the generated isolation suite cannot catch it: there is no cross-tenant SELECT to fail on. The scope is passed at the CALL SITE rather than defaulted inside the function, so the question being asked is visible in the model. Applied to employee.id_number_hash and, for the identical reason, retrofitted to employer_bank_account.account_number_hash - an unscoped digest there would have said that two subscribers bank into the same account. Safe to retrofit because there is no data; after launch it would need every hash recomputed, and a stale hash does not error, it silently reports that everybody is new. |
| D-96 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | The ID number is validated by checksum AND by cross-checking the date of birth, and both must pass before id_number_verified is set | The Luhn check digit catches a single mistyped digit and most transpositions, which is most capture errors. It cannot catch a number that is internally perfect and belongs to somebody else, or a date of birth typed from the wrong line of the same document - and both of those reach SARS looking correct. The first six digits of the ID number and the captured date of birth are two independent records of the same fact, so comparing them is free and catches what neither field can catch alone. The century is deliberately NOT guessed: 900101 is 1990 or 2090 and the number does not say, so employees/identity.py returns both candidates and lets the captured date decide, which turns the ambiguity into the cross-check. id_number_verified stays FALSE for a passport, an asylum permit and a work permit, and means 'nobody has verified this' rather than 'this is wrong' - there is no checksum on those for this system to check, and an employer holding one must still be able to run payroll. |
| D-97 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | The ID number unique constraint is per TENANT, not global | A domestic worker employed by two households is two employment relationships with two unrelated subscribers, and in this market that is the ordinary case rather than an edge one. A global unique would make the second employer unable to capture their own employee, and the error message would tell each of them something true about the other. Scoping the hash (D-95) already makes the digests differ across tenants; the constraint states the intent so nobody later 'fixes' it into a global one. |
| D-98 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | employee.email is a plain EmailField lower-cased on save, not CITEXT. WORKBOOK CHANGE | Sheet 02 specifies CITEXT. Django deprecated its CITEXT field classes in 4.2 in favour of case-insensitive collations, and a nondeterministic collation breaks LIKE and index-backed prefix search - the same trade D-17 refused on the sort columns, where the answer was to lower-case in the expression instead. employee.email is not unique, so case-insensitivity buys only matching convenience, and normalising on write gives that without the collation. app_user.email already works this way, so the two now agree. |
| D-99 | 13 Sep 2026 | Security | A plain save() on an EXISTING row with no tenant pinned fails as an INSERT, and CLAUDE.md's table now says so | Found while writing the employee tests. The documented trap said plain save() silently writes nothing, which is true for a NEW row. For a row that already exists, Django's UPDATE matches zero rows under the policy, Django reads zero affected rows as 'this row is not in the table yet', and falls back to an INSERT with the primary key set - which the policy then refuses. The message names the policy and the insert and mentions neither the update nor the missing context, so it reads as though the row is being created twice. refresh_from_db() on the same row raises DoesNotExist for an object you are holding. Both added to the table in CLAUDE.md; six failure modes now, two of which say what is actually wrong. |
| D-100 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | The BCEA s43 minimum employment age is a statutory_parameter row with a citation, not a literal 15 in employees/. NEW PARAMETER and a new unit | CLAUDE.md's rule is 'never hard-code a rate, threshold, multiplier or leave rule', and an age below which a person may not be employed is a threshold. The automated guard would NOT have caught a literal here: test_no_hardcoded_rates scans for Decimal and float constants, and an age is an int - the rule is about statutory figures, not about a Python type, and this is the first case where those two come apart. MINIMUM_EMPLOYMENT_AGE = 15 years, effective from the commencement of the Act rather than from 1 March 2026, because the threshold has applied continuously since then and a back-dated engagement must resolve against it too. statutory_parameter.Unit gains YEARS. Loaded as reference/ref-2026.03.01-employment.json and added to FIXTURE_ORDER. |
| D-101 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | The minimum-age check REFUSES when the reference data is absent, which is the opposite of how PayGroup.clean() handles the same situation | PayGroup.clean() skips validation when no working-time rule set is loaded, because refusing would block onboarding for a reason the employer cannot act on, and the staleness guard blocks the payroll run further along anyway. There is no such backstop on an engagement, and the failure mode is not a wrong figure on a payslip: BCEA s43(1) forbids employing a child and s43(3) makes it an OFFENCE committed by the employer, with this system having recorded the engagement. So check_minimum_age raises rather than assuming a figure, and the message names the command that loads it. The two opposite behaviours are deliberate and both are documented at their call sites. |
| D-102 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | Only the first limb of BCEA s43(1) is encoded, and the fixture's notes say so in capitals | s43(1) forbids work by a child who is under 15 OR under the minimum school-leaving age in terms of any law - the operative floor is the HIGHER of the two. Today they coincide: South African Schools Act 84 of 1996 s3(1) requires attendance until the last school day of the year in which the learner turns 15, or the ninth grade, whichever comes first. So one figure is correct today and would silently become wrong if the school-leaving age moved, with nothing in the schema noticing. Recorded in the row's notes rather than modelled as two parameters, because a second parameter that always equals the first is a column nobody maintains. s43(2) - work inappropriate for the child's age, or risking their well-being, education, health or development - is deliberately NOT encoded at all: it is a judgement about the work, not a number. |
| D-103 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | A re-hire is a NEW engagement row, and employee_position carries an EXCLUDE over its date range rather than only a unique on the start date | Service length is read from employee_engagement, so an employee who left in 2027 and returned in 2029 must have two rows. Capturing the second by editing the first would grant a longer notice period, more accrued leave and a larger severance figure - all of them plausible on screen, none of them earned. engage() refuses a second open engagement and refuses a start date inside a previous period, and the partial unique on is_current backs it for any writer that does not come through the service function. On employee_position the unique on (employee, effective_from) only stops two positions STARTING on one day; a back-dated promotion starts inside the open period and slips past it, leaving two job grades in force, so two minimum wages, with the ORM picking one by ordering. Only the exclusion constraint sees that. |
| D-104 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | BCEA s35's four-and-one-third factor is a statutory_parameter row, and employees/rates.py is handed it rather than knowing it | s35 sets monthly remuneration at four and one-third times the weekly wage, so every conversion between pay bases passes through it - a monthly salary cannot become an hourly rate without it. Written as Decimal('4.333333') in the derivation it would have been a gazetted figure hiding in arithmetic. MONTHLY_TO_WEEKLY_FACTOR, unit ratio, effective from the Act's commencement, cited to s35 read with the Department's own Form BCEA1A summary. The Act says 'four and one-third', which is exactly 13/3; the stored value is that rounded to the column's six decimal places, an error under one part in ten million and well inside invariant 6's working precision. It is NOT 52/12 by coincidence - the same number, but the citation is s35 rather than the calendar. SD1's '4,333 weeks' December bonus is this factor at three decimals as that gazette published it, and stays a separate figure in its own rule set row. NOTE: it went into the employment fixture first and the LOADER REFUSED the second load of an already-loaded version, exactly as designed - which produced the better structure, a separate ref-2026.03.01-remuneration.json, since eligibility and rate derivation are different subjects. |
| D-105 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | The minimum wage hours band is PASSED IN and defaults to 'all'. The 27-hour split is not computed from an employee's hours | employees/remuneration.py first derived the band with a literal Decimal(27), and test_no_hardcoded_rates caught it - correctly, because 27 is a gazetted threshold from the old Sectoral Determination 7 wage tables and not arithmetic. It was NOT replaced with a reference-data lookup, because no loaded rate uses a band: all four minimum_wage_rate rows are 'all', domestic workers having been brought to National Minimum Wage parity. Creating a parameter would have meant citing a threshold nobody has confirmed is still live. So the caller states the band, resolve's fallback order returns the 'all' row for every sector with no banded rows, and automatic band selection waits for whoever loads banded rates with a gazette in hand. The guard working as intended, and the honest answer being to remove the figure rather than to file it. |
| D-106 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | Weekly is the HUB of rate derivation: every basis converts to weekly first, and hourly and daily both come off that | Deriving daily as hourly x hours_per_day gives a different answer from weekly / days_per_week the moment hours_per_day x days_per_week does not equal hours_per_week - which is what an employer produces by changing one field and not the other. The employee would then be paid one figure for a day of leave and another for a day of work, both of them 'correct'. Weekly as the hub makes the two agree by construction. The captured basis is preserved exactly rather than round-tripped: an employee captured at R4,500 a month has derived_monthly_rate of exactly 4500, because their contract says 4,500 and a round trip lands a few cents away. The three derived rates are STORED columns rather than properties, per invariant 2: recomputing on read would recompute with today's hours and today's statutory factor, so a 2029 re-run of March 2026 would quietly differ from the payslip the employee was given. |
| D-107 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | The pay cache is refreshed as at TODAY, not as at the new row's effective date, and capture() takes an explicit as_at | Found by the test written for D-18's second half. capture() first refreshed employee.current_pay_group and current_pay_basis using the new row's own effective_from - so a rate captured in March to start in July moved the cache to July's pay group the moment it was saved, and the employee list would have grouped four months of payroll under a pay group that does not apply yet. That is exactly the bug the nightly job exists to catch, written into the write path. The date is an explicit argument defaulting to today rather than a clock read inside, so a test can state the day it means. |
| D-108 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | Below the minimum wage RAISES, and can be accepted by a named user whose id is stored | Sheet 02 makes is_below_minimum a flag with below_minimum_ack_by_user_id beside it, and PHASES requires a visible logged exception. Both: capture() raises BelowMinimumWageError carrying the offered rate, the floor and the gazette citation, and proceeds only when handed an acknowledging user, which is then stored. A hard block would lock an employer out of their own record part-way through correcting a typo; a silent flag would let underpayment through with nobody's name on it, and 'the system let me' is not a defence at the CCMA. The comparison is on the DERIVED HOURLY rate, because minimum_wage_rate.hourly_rate is the authoritative gazette column and the gazetted monthly figure is stored as published and rounds. An acknowledgement is NOT stored on a rate that clears, since a stray ack would later read as though somebody had waved something through. |
| D-109 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | work_schedule, work_schedule_day, employee_tax_profile and employee_bank_account all carry an EXCLUDE over their date range | Same reason as employee_remuneration, and the consequences differ per table. Two work schedules in force means a day is both an ordinary working day and not one, which decides whether a public holiday is paid unworked (BCEA s18), which Sunday multiplier applies (s16 pays double time only when Sunday is NOT ordinarily worked), how many days a period of leave consumes, and what a day of notice is worth. Two tax profiles means two directive percentages. Two live bank accounts means the payment file picks one by row order. In every case the unique on the start date stops two rows BEGINNING on one day and does nothing about a back-dated row that starts inside an open period. |
| D-110 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | A statutory THRESHOLD that only ever answers a yes/no question is stored as a DECLARED BOOLEAN on the employee's record, not as a figure anywhere | This is the resolution to D-105 and it came from the workbook. work_schedule.works_over_27_hours_week is the SD7 rate band selector: the employer states which side of the threshold the person is on, the gazette keeps the 27, and no number lives in code OR in reference data. Better than either of the alternatives - a literal is a hard-coded rate, and a statutory_parameter would have meant citing a threshold nobody has confirmed is still live. The same shape applies to employee_tax_profile.is_uif_exempt with uif_exempt_reason 'under_24_hours_month': the Unemployment Insurance Act keeps its 24-hour threshold, the employer states the fact. The rule to carry forward: when a statute's number only ever decides a yes/no that a human already knows the answer to, capture the answer and not the number. A contradictory capture - 45 ordinary hours a week with the under-27 band - is honoured exactly as given, because nothing in this system is entitled to overrule the employer on a threshold it does not hold. |
| D-111 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | employee_bank_account gains account_number_hash, which sheet 02 does not have. WORKBOOK ADDITION | Several 'employees' paid into one bank account is the classic payroll fraud in contract cleaning, which is half this product's market, and without a comparable column the question cannot be asked at all. Keyed HMAC scoped to the tenant, exactly as on employer_bank_account and employee.id_number (D-95). It is a SIGNAL, NOT A BLOCK: spouses and families legitimately share an account, so a system that refused the second one would be wrong more often than right - the rows are findable and neither is rejected. Added now rather than later because there is no data; after launch every row would need backfilling, and a stale hash does not error, it reports that everybody is unique. |
| D-112 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employees | A CHECK constraint on an encrypted column is refused by the field, and the companion column is what the constraint reads | The EFT-needs-an-account-number check was written against account_number and EncryptedCharField refused it outright - Fernet ciphertext is non-deterministic, so comparing it to anything is meaningless, and the field raises rather than letting a constraint that can never fire look like protection (D-77). The check is now on account_number_last4, which is plain text, derived from the number on every save, and empty exactly when the number is. Worth recording because the refusal arrives from the FIELD during makemigrations, which reads as a tooling error rather than as the design working. |
| D-113 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | night_allowance_value stays NUMERIC with zero meaning 'the statute names no figure'. Raised in verification, reviewed, and kept — with a BLOCKING check added so the ambiguity cannot become an underpayment | Kobus marked the domestic zero WRONG in the first verification pass and proposed 'by_agreement' as the value. He is right about the risk and the column cannot hold a string; night_allowance_type already carries 'by_agreement' and he passed that row. So the pair already says what the statute says - BCEA s17(2) requires night work to be compensated by an allowance or reduced hours and deliberately names no amount - and the decision after review was to leave the column as it is rather than make it nullable and re-issue the fixture. What that leaves is a zero with TWO meanings, told apart only by the type column: 'no statutory figure exists' against 'the gazetted figure is nil'. check_night_allowance_coherence() is the guard, and it is blocking in both directions. A type of percentage or fixed_amount with a value of zero tells a calculator that night work is compensated at nothing, and it looks like data rather than like a mistake. A type of by_agreement carrying a real figure means one column was edited and the other was not - the likelier accident, since SD1 gazettes ten percent for contract cleaning and domestic does not. The claimed citation, SD7 clause 4(3), could NOT be confirmed: the consolidated SD7 reachable online has no dedicated night work allowance clause at all. If the gazette does carry one, the figure it names should be loaded and this row superseded. |
| D-114 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Source codes 3901 and 3907 loaded from SARS's own 2026 code guide, and SEVERANCE activated. CLOSES D-90 | PAYE-AE-06-G06 revision 13, effective 19 September 2025, supplied by Kobus. D-70 left 3901 out because nobody had the guide; the guide arrived. 3901 is 'Gratuities / Severance Benefits (Subject to PAYE)' and its third qualifying limb - 'services terminated due to reduction of personnel or employer ceased trading' - is dismissal for operational requirements, the only ground on which BCEA s41 severance is due, matching EmployeeEngagement.SEVERANCE_REASONS exactly. 3907 is loaded ALONGSIDE because leaving it out is the trap: the guide says a paragraph (d) lump sum which is NOT a severance benefit MUST be reflected under 3907, so with only 3901 available a resignation gratuity or a retirement lump sum below 55 would be misfiled onto the severance code. The base flags are derived, each from its own statute rather than from SARS: taxable True (the guide says Subject to PAYE); UIF base False because UICA 4 of 2002 s1 excludes paragraph (a), (cA), (d), (e), (eA) and (eD) gross income amounts and SARS places both codes in paragraph (d) - the same subsection that puts commission outside the UIF base; SDL base False because SDLA 9 of 1999 s3, as set out in SARS's SDL employer guide, excludes paragraph (a), (d), (e) and (eA) likewise. COIDA base False is a READING AND NOT A CITATION, flagged VERIFY in the row's notes, because no source was found naming termination lump sums in the COIDA earnings definition. |
| D-115 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employers | Activating a system payroll component is a named maintenance action that opens the reference-maintenance escape hatch, not a workaround of the system-row lock | SEVERANCE was created inactive before 3901 existed, and seeding never updates an existing row - so activating it needs an UPDATE, which the system-row lock refuses even under platform_context() by design (D-93), because every tenant reads these rows. seed_system_components(activate_severance=True) opens labourmax.allow_reference_maintenance by name, saves, and closes it. The two alternatives were both worse: dropping and re-creating the row would orphan anything already pointing at it, and relaxing the trigger would quietly make every system component editable forever. IT ALSO HIT D-92 ON THE FIRST RUN and failed with the lock's own message: the set_config committed on its own under autocommit and the flag was gone before the UPDATE. transaction.atomic() first, then platform_context(), then the flag - all three are transaction-scoped and all three are needed. Second time that trap has bitten in this codebase. |
| D-116 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | The eight deduction and total source codes carry no base flags BY CONSTRUCTION, and the verification workbook's column heading was what made that look wrong | Kobus queried all eight in the first verification pass - 4001, 4003, 4005, 4102, 4116, 4141, 4142, 4149 - because a row reading 'PAYE False' against code 4102, which IS PAYE, reads as an error. It is not: the flag says whether the amount is part of the base the levy is computed ON, and nothing is ever computed FROM a deduction or a total. That is the same invariant check_source_codes has enforced since D-71, precisely to stop UIF being charged on the UIF figure. The data was right and the workbook was wrong. The column is now headed 'Is this amount IN the base for...' with the 4102 case spelled out in the sheet blurb. SARS's 2026 guide confirms all eight as valid deduction and employees' tax codes for the year, and confirms 3607 is a main code again from 2020 - it was folded into 3601 for 2010 to 2019 - so its separate use in 2026 is correct. The guide also shows 3615, director's remuneration, dead from 2019, which is consistent with directors using the ordinary tables and the ordinary income code. |
| D-117 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | Kobus signed off 3901 and 3907, which clears the Priority sheet to 22 of 22. That is an OWNER CONFIRMATION and it is NOT the second-person verification, and it is NOT a legal opinion on the COIDA flag | The two lump sum codes were the last VERIFY left on the priority queue after D-113, D-114 and D-116 resolved the rest, so the sheet the workbook exists to empty is now empty. Worth recording precisely because "the priority sheet is clear" will read later as "the data is verified", and it is not. Three separate things are being confused and each has a different owner. (1) The SARS wording, the code group and the taxable flag are SARS's own and Kobus has now agreed them against the guide he supplied - that is done. (2) The UIF and SDL flags are derived from UICA 4 of 2002 s1 and SDLA 9 of 1999 s3 and stand on those citations, not on anybody's agreement. (3) The COIDA flag is still a reading with no source behind it, and an owner ticking Y on a row whose own note says READING, NOT A CITATION does not convert it into one - it records that he read the reasoning and found it plausible, which is worth having and is not evidence. Escalated as O-15 rather than left inside the row's notes, because a VERIFY buried in a fixture's notes column is found by nobody. Separately, the loaded versions all still report NOT verified: verifystatutory is a whole-version sign-off by a second person who has checked every figure, and 216 of the 281 workbook rows have not been looked at by anyone but the person who loaded them. See O-16. |
| D-118 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | The contract cleaning areas are A (the listed Metropolitan AND Local Councils, one column), B (all of KwaZulu-Natal, BCCCI, no gazetted figure) and C (the rest of the RSA, a residual). SUPERSEDES D-61, and municipality_area_map is loaded with the twelve names the determination itself prints | RECONSTRUCTED 13 Sep 2026 from statutory/models.py and reference/ref-2026.03.01.json after this row was destroyed by a forced overwrite of DECISIONS.md from a stale copy - the code and the fixture survived, the record did not, and the wording below is rebuilt from them rather than from the original. Restore any detail that is missing. GN R.7083, GG 54075, 3 February 2026 prints the rates as three columns. D-61 read the table as A = metros, B = named local councils, C = KwaZulu-Natal, and overrode the model's own docstring to say so. D-61 WAS WRONG. The five Metropolitan Councils and the seven Local Councils share ONE column at the high rate - they are not two areas - so reading them apart underpaid every Area A local council by R2,94 an hour. KwaZulu-Natal is Area B and the gazette gives it no figure at all, pointing instead at the collective agreement of the Bargaining Council for the Contract Cleaning Service Industry, which is what uses_bargaining_council_rates records: the loader must know a different source applies and that those citations are an agreement clause rather than a gazette number. Area C is 'in the rest of the RSA' at the low rate - a RESIDUAL and not a list, and therefore where most workplaces land, which is why treating KwaZulu-Natal as C left the whole country without a rate. municipality_area_map holds ONLY the twelve municipalities the determination names, under the gazette's own spelling: Area B is a province and Area C is a residual, so neither is a list and neither has rows. The stored names are deliberately out of date - Randfontein and Westonaria merged into Rand West City after the August 2016 elections and Greater East Rand Metro has been Ekurhuleni since 2000 - because the determination says what it says and that is what a verifier checks against. Mapping today's municipal names onto them is a presentation problem, not a reference-data one. |
| D-119 | 13 Sep 2026 | Go-to-market | BOTH sectors launch together. O-02 closed against the recommendation, which makes three known gaps into launch blockers rather than later work | The recommendation was domestic first, on the grounds that it has no area rates, no bargaining council and no statutory bonus. Kobus has decided both, which is his call and is defensible - the contract cleaning schema is already built, and a cleaning company is worth roughly ten domestic employers in revenue. What the decision does is move work forward rather than remove it, and the three items must now be tracked as blocking: municipality_area_map is EMPTY, so a cleaning employer cannot be onboarded at all until every municipality is mapped to its BCCCI area; Area C (KwaZulu-Natal) rates are not loaded; and D-68's notice band gap - SD1's four-week boundary sitting differently from the BCEA's - is unresolved and was scheduled 'before P6'. None of these is hard. All three are now on the critical path, and the failure mode is discovering that in P6 with a launch date already announced. |
| D-120 | 13 Sep 2026 | Commercial | Trial is 7 days with a card taken upfront, charged on day 8 unless cancelled.
