O-05 closed against the recommendation | The recommendation was 14 days without a card, trading incomplete accounts for lower friction. Kobus has chosen the opposite trade: fewer signups, but every signup that completes is a real intent to buy, and nobody has to be chased for a card at the moment they are least engaged. It also composes exactly with D-37, which already specified the day-8 charge as the lesser of the disclosed amount and the actual headcount amount - so the billing side of this was designed for a card-upfront trial before the trial length was settled. ONE THING FOR THE LAWYER, not a blocker: a card-upfront trial that converts automatically is the shape South African consumer protection law is most attentive to, and the cancellation route must be as easy to find as the signup was. D-121 is what answers that, and the two should be reviewed together. |
| D-121 | 13 Sep 2026 | Billing | Cancellation is a button inside the product with a free-text feedback box, and it stops all further payments. Access then runs to the end of the paid period and drops to READ-ONLY rather than closing | Kobus's answer settles the billing half: a self-service button, no email-us, no retention gauntlet - which is also what pairs safely with D-120's card-upfront trial. What it did not settle is what happens to ACCESS, and that cannot be left implicit because the answer is constrained by law rather than by preference. No further payments implies no pro-rata refund for the month already paid, so access must run to the end of that period or the employer has paid for days they cannot use. After that it goes read-only rather than dark, because the employer remains legally obliged to keep payroll records for five years under the Tax Administration Act and cannot discharge that obligation from a closed account. Deleting on cancellation would make this product the reason an employer fails a SARS audit. The read-only period and the POPIA deletion obligation pull against each other and the five-year retention carve-out is what reconciles them - already noted in O-12 and to be written into the operator agreement. |
| D-122 | 13 Sep 2026 | Employee data | Bulk employee import IS in scope for v1, with a downloadable .xlsx template. O-11 closed yes, and it lands in P4 | A cleaning company onboarding forty staff will not type forty forms, and D-119 has just made contract cleaning a launch sector rather than a later one - so this stopped being optional in the same breath. The template is downloadable and generated from the live schema rather than maintained by hand, because a template that drifts from the columns the importer expects produces failures the employer cannot diagnose. Same preview-validate-apply-reverse pattern as the attendance import: nothing is written until the employer has seen what will be written, and any applied batch can be reversed as a unit. Roughly a week, and it adds employee_import_batch to P4's scope. The import must run every validation the individual capture path runs - the ID check, the minimum age check, the minimum wage check with its acknowledgement - because an import that bypasses them is a way to put forty below-minimum employees into the system with nobody's name against the exception. |
| D-123 | 13 Sep 2026 | POPIA | Hosting and operation are SOUTH AFRICA ONLY. This is an architectural constraint, not a preference, and it removes the s72 transborder problem rather than the sub-operator register | Kobus's answer to O-14. Recorded as a decision because it will otherwise be violated silently and cheaply: every default in modern tooling is a US region, and the first time somebody adds an email sender, an SMS gateway, error tracking or an analytics pixel without checking, the constraint is gone and nobody notices. Hosting must be a South African region - af-south-1 in Cape Town, Azure South Africa North, or a local provider - and EVERY vendor that touches employee data must be checked for where it processes, not only where it is billed. What the answer does is dispose of POPIA s72, which is the hard part of transborder flows, since there is no transborder flow. What it does NOT do is dispose of the register itself: O-14 stays open, because the register's other job is answering an employer's 'who else touches our data', and that question is asked by employers whether or not the answer crosses a border. |
| D-124 | 13 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | The COIDA earnings instrument is GOVERNMENT NOTICE 1843 of 2023, not Schedule 4. Kobus's answer reached the right conclusion on a citation that does not check out - and the notice CONFIRMS the overtime flag we already have | Kobus answered O-15 'NO' and cited 'Schedule 4 of COIDA', quoting a definition excluding 'payments for overtime, commission not related to regular work, and payments made upon termination of service'. Chased down, the instrument that actually governs the return of earnings is Government Notice 1843 of 2023, Government Gazette 48673, 30 May 2023, made under s82(1)(a), s83(2)(a) and s83(8) - which is a real find and is what three earlier searches failed to reach. On its own terms the quoted line is wrong in two places and right in one. WRONG ON OVERTIME: the notice includes 'Overtime of a regular nature not intermittent or irregular overtime', so regular overtime is INSIDE the base and the loaded is_coida_remuneration True on 3607 is correct - the quoted exclusion is s63's intermittent-overtime rule, which governs what an INJURED WORKER IS PAID and not what an employer DECLARES, two different calculations the Act keeps apart. Same for commission, included expressly 'even though the amount may vary from month to month'. RIGHT ON THE CONCLUSION, WRONG ON THE AUTHORITY: 'payments made upon termination of service' appears NOWHERE in the notice's exclusion list, which runs reimbursive payments, non-recurring tasks outside normal duties, intangible fringe benefits, special expenses, occasional allowances and directors' profit sharing. So the False on 3901 and 3907 is still a reading. It is a much better documented reading than it was this morning, and the question left is narrow enough to put to a lawyer in one sentence - see O-15. The general lesson is the one CLAUDE.md already states: a source that agrees with the conclusion is not thereby a source. |
| D-125 | 13 Sep 2026 | Pricing | Every price is now settled. Domestic R180 including 2 active employees then R50 a head; contract cleaning R380 for band 1 then R40 a head above 5, with D-35's six bands KEPT and over-100 still routed to contact sales. SUPERSEDES D-33 | O-03 closed. Domestic: the included headcount drops from three to two, which is the one place Kobus's answer overrode a settled decision and he confirmed it deliberately - so D-33 is superseded rather than contradicted, and a three-employee domestic account now pays R230 instead of R180. Contract cleaning: the answer as written collapsed D-35's six bands into one flat formula, which would have let a 400-cleaner company self-checkout at R16,180 a month with nobody from LabourMax ever speaking to them; the bands are kept and the numbers apply across them. Bands 2 to 5 are MECHANICALLY IDENTICAL under D-34's formula - 380 + (headcount - 5) x 40 for all of them - and they stay as separate plan_price_band rows anyway, because the whole point of bands being data is repricing one of them without a release, and rows that do not yet differ are free while rows that do not exist need a migration. The resulting curve: 5 cleaners R380, 6 R420, 10 R580, 30 R1,380, 50 R2,180, 100 R4,180, above that a conversation. Every boundary costs one extra person rather than a step, which is what D-34 was for. |
| D-126 | 13 Sep 2026 | Brand | LabourMax is a SEPARATE REGISTERED COMPANY, not a trading name of Primax SA. O-08 closed, and it has to happen before the first invoice rather than before launch | The billing module writes the issuing entity into every tax invoice, credit note and statement it produces, and a tax invoice naming the wrong entity is not merely untidy - it is invalid, and the customer cannot claim the input VAT on it. So the answer had to be settled before the invoice template is built, which is P1. What it now requires, in order: CIPC registration of the company; its own bank account, because the gateway settles to the entity named on the invoice and O-04 is still open; VAT registration once turnover passes the R1 million compulsory threshold, with voluntary registration available earlier and worth considering, since B2B customers claiming input VAT prefer a VAT-registered supplier and cleaning companies are all B2B; and its own PAYE and UIF registration the moment it employs anybody. It also settles a POPIA question that O-12 was going to have to answer anyway: the operator named in every operator agreement is LabourMax (Pty) Ltd, not Primax, and if any Primax staff or systems touch tenant data then Primax is a SUB-OPERATOR and belongs in O-14's register like any other vendor. Solo founder today makes that look academic; it is exactly the thing that is impossible to reconstruct in year three. |
| D-127 | 14 Sep 2026 | Leave | ``leave_type`` is built in P4, out of phase, because ``employee_leave_entitlement`` cannot point at a table that does not exist. One table forward, not the domain | P4's entitlement row records that THIS employee gets something other than the statute allows for THIS kind of leave, so it is meaningless without a leave type to name. Both alternatives were worse. Leaving the entitlement table to P6 would have closed P4 with a hole in the employee master file and forced P6 to reopen P4's migrations. Pointing the FK at a string code would have put an unenforceable reference in the schema, which is the exact failure PROTECT exists to prevent, and the one FORCE RLS already makes invisible (D-76). Only ``leave_type`` moves: cycles, the transaction ledger, applications and the accrual engine stay in P6. It is the SECOND shared-tenancy table - a NULL tenant means every employer may read it, the platform stocks the statutory types and an employer adds a birthday day or a long-service day on top - so ``enable_rls_shared`` plus the system-row lock, same shape as ``payroll_component`` (D-87, D-93). NO ENTITLEMENT FIGURE LIVES ON IT: the number of days is statutory and stays in ``leave_rule_set`` with its citation. What the table carries is the SHAPE of a type - whether it accrues, whether it is paid, whether it survives termination. ``cycle_months`` looks like a rate and is not: 12 for annual, 36 for sick, a property of the cycle rather than an amount of anything, and the days accruing inside it are still read from the rule set. |
| D-128 | 14 Sep 2026 | Employees | Whether a deduction needs written consent under BCEA s34 is DERIVED from ``calculation_method``, not from ``is_system`` and not from a new column | s34(1) permits a deduction without the employee's written consent only where a law, court order, arbitration award or collective agreement requires it. Sheet 02 gives ``payroll_component`` no column for that and this build does not add one, so the test is: a deduction whose calculation_method is STATUTORY is computed from reference data because a statute says so, and needs no signature. PAYE and UIF_EE are STATUTORY; ACCOM_DED and ADVANCE_DED are FIXED and both need one. ``is_system`` IS THE WRONG SIGNAL AND THE MISTAKE IS WORTH NAMING, because all four are system components and it reads as though it means 'required by law' - using it would have waved through an accommodation deduction and a loan repayment with nobody's signature on either, which is precisely the pair s34 was written about. The per-component ceiling (``total_deduction_cap_pct``, e.g. the ten percent an accommodation deduction may not exceed) is enforced on the row. The ACROSS-ALL-COMPONENTS s34 limit is not and cannot be: no single row can see the total, which is how an employer ends up with four individually lawful deductions that together take three quarters of a wage. That check belongs to the payroll run and is P7's. |
| D-132 | 14 Sep 2026 | Employees | A termination captured in advance moves NOTHING until its date. is_current, status and is_billable are as-at columns recomputed by employees/currentstate.py, on write and by a nightly job | terminate() set is_current false, status TERMINATED and is_billable false at the moment of capture - so an employee serving a month's notice read as having already left, six weeks before they stopped working. The model's own docstring said the opposite ('a future-dated termination is captured in advance and the employee is still currently employed until it arrives'), and the consequences are not cosmetic: any current-employee query, including the payroll run that still owes a final salary, a leave payout and an IRP5, would skip them; billing would stop counting a subscription still in use. It is D-107 pointing the other way - the cache moved on capture rather than on the date. The CHECK 'is_current implies no termination date' was dropped with it (migration employees/0006), because a future-dated termination is exactly the case where both are true; it is NOT replaced with a date-aware CHECK, because 'termination_date >= CURRENT_DATE' gets STRICTER as time passes and a row valid when written would fail the next restore or table rewrite - the opposite direction to employee_born_before_today. What must hold is one current engagement per employee, which is the partial unique, untouched. Two guards moved with it: 'already engaged' in engage() and 'no open engagement' in capture() now read termination_date IS NULL rather than is_current, or a re-hire arranged in advance would be refused and an employee engaged from next Monday could not have an opening rate captured today. NUMBERING: D-129 to D-131 are used by the parallel session's CLAUDE.md and are not in this register - see O-18. |
| D-133 | 14 Sep 2026 | Security | A user's own view choices live in tenant_membership.ui_preferences, a JSONB bag with a registry. AN ADDITION TO SHEET 02 | P4 asks for the employee list sort to be remembered per user and the workbook has no table for it. The membership IS 'this person in this tenant', it is already tenant-scoped and policy-covered, and a table per UI preference is a migration for every screen decision. The bag is not open: employees/listing.py holds the permitted keys and their permitted values and refuses anything else, because a bag that accepts anything is where a setting that decides a figure eventually lands - a per-user copy of a compliance decision with no citation and no audit trail (D-89). It does not weaken D-16 either: the employer's EMPLOYEE_LIST_SORT still governs the attendance grid and the payroll run, and is still this user's default until they choose otherwise. |
| D-134 | 14 Sep 2026 | Leave | leave_type gains the is_system column D-127 said it had, plus the shared-row/system-row CHECK pair | The table shipped with lock_system_rows('leave_type') installed and no is_system column, so the trigger's first statement - IF NOT OLD.is_system - raised on EVERY update and delete of EVERY row. An employer could add 'Birthday leave' and then never rename or remove it; the platform could not correct a typo in the catalogue. It survived a green suite because the single test covering the area asserted DatabaseError and 'record old has no field is_system' IS a DatabaseError: the test passed on the wrong exception. Every refusal test here now asserts on the message as well as the class. The CHECK pair is payroll_component's, for payroll_component's reason: a shared row with is_system false would be readable by every tenant and deletable by any of them, because a DELETE is checked against the policy's USING clause only (D-93). A colour CHECK came with it - the value lands in a leave calendar and in a PDF, and 'blue' renders as a browser guess in one and as black in the other, which reads as a broken document rather than as a bad setting. |
| D-135 | 14 Sep 2026 | Employees | O-17 RESOLVED: keep balance_outstanding and total_deduction_cap_pct (sheet 02 wins), but employee_recurring_component loses its EXCLUDE and gains a UniqueConstraint instead. Migration employees/0007 | O-17's two columns are settled by the workbook rule that already governs every other disagreement with sheet 02: it wins until Kobus says otherwise, and both columns are in it. That leaves the schema judgement O-18 flagged as separate from O-17, and it is decided the other way. The parallel session's EXCLUDE - one live row per (employee, payroll_component) at a time - is wrong for a reason sheet 03 does not state and only the s34 total exposes: two advances of the same component running at once is ordinary, and forbidding it makes an employer invent a second component so the deduction can coexist, at which point it stops being recognisable as an advance in the BCEA s34 total - a compliance failure no test catches. What the EXCLUDE was really standing in for is a line captured twice, so it is replaced with a UniqueConstraint over (employee, payroll_component, effective_from, amount, percentage_of_basic). nulls_distinct=False matters because amount and percentage_of_basic are mutually exclusive and therefore nullable, and NULL = NULL is unknown in PostgreSQL - without it two rows with the same NULL amount would not collide. |
| D-136 | 14 Sep 2026 | Employees | employee_leave_entitlement carries an EXCLUDE over [effective_from, effective_to) per (employee, leave_type). A WORKBOOK DEVIATION, formalised rather than newly built - it shipped in employees/0005 | Sheet 03 is silent on this table but asks for exactly this shape on employee_position and work_schedule, which is the deviation's justification: an effective-dated row whose scope is a foreign key, not the whole table, needs an exclusion rather than a plain unique. It went unrecorded because it was written under the parallel session's numbering (see O-18) rather than this register's - the constraint itself was never wrong, only its citation was missing. The unique on effective_from alone stops two grants beginning on one day and does nothing about a grant back-dated into an open period, which leaves two entitlements in force for one leave type with nothing but row order deciding between them - the D-103 trap again. effective_to is EXCLUSIVE, matching every other effective-dated range in this schema. |
| D-137 | 14 Sep 2026 | Environment | manage.py dbcheck's REQUIRED_EXTENSIONS is reduced to btree_gist alone | citext and pgcrypto were required and nothing in the repo calls either. btree_gist is the one extension actually in use - it backs every ExclusionConstraint in this schema and is installed by statutory/0002. pgcrypto has no caller because hashing and encryption are Python-side (hashlib, hmac, Fernet), which is what D-77 keeping the key out of the database means in practice: there is no SQL-side crypto function to need pgcrypto for. citext appears nowhere but this tuple - D-98 already abandoned it for employee.email in favour of lower-casing on save, and nothing else in the schema ever asked for it. The list is not a wishlist: the migration that needs an extension is the enforcement point, so REQUIRED_EXTENSIONS names only what a migration has actually installed, and grows with the next migration that adds one rather than ahead of it. Without this, a correctly rebuilt dev database - one with no hand-installed extensions lying around from an earlier, different schema - fails dbcheck for two extensions nothing needs, which reads as a broken environment rather than as a stale check. |
| D-138 | 14 Sep 2026 | Documents | document_category is the THIRD shared table, and it carries is_system from its first migration rather than sheet 02's column list | Same shape as payroll_component (D-87) and leave_type (D-127): a NULL tenant_id means the platform stocks this for every employer, the opposite of what the same NULL means on TenantOptionalModel. Sheet 02 gives the table no is_system column, and D-134 is the reason it needs one anyway: lock_system_rows() keys on is_system, and installing that trigger on a table without the column makes every UPDATE and DELETE on the table fail inside it - a tenant renaming its own category included, not only an attempt to edit a system row. leave_type shipped the trigger before the column and needed a second migration to close the gap it opened; document_category ships both together in documents/0001_initial, so the bug D-134 fixed has no window to exist here. The shared-row/system-row CHECK pair is payroll_component's and leave_type's, for the same reason (D-93): without it a shared row with is_system false is readable by every tenant and deletable by any of them, because a DELETE is checked against the policy's USING clause only. |

Public holidays follow s18(2), which pays double for the whole day worked, not only the excess over some cap - unlike an ordinary day, there is no daily statutory ceiling that turns the tail of a long public-holiday shift into a different rate. Fabricating one from this codebase's own arithmetic and calling it golden would prove nothing: a golden test exists to catch the calculator disagreeing with an INDEPENDENT authority, and a test that reproduces the code that produced it can only ever agree with itself, forever, even after the code becomes wrong. Coverage instead: table-driven tests for every bucket and the boundaries the task named explicitly (exactly ordinary hours, one minute over, a night-window-crossing shift, both Sunday cases, both public-holiday cases, a short day, standby with and without work), Hypothesis property tests for the invariants that must hold regardless of input (no bucket negative, the worked total never exceeds 24 hours, ordinary never exceeds the schedule, overtime is exactly the excess, a day with no work produces no paid hours but the guarantee), and 100% branch coverage. If a published worked example is ever found, this decision is what should be revisited first. |

| D-158 | 14 Sep 2026, CORRECTED 15 Sep 2026 | Statutory data | CORRECTED. Inclusivity at a `termination_notice_band` boundary is DATA, per boundary, read from each statute's own wording — not a global convention. Two new columns, `service_from_inclusive` and `service_to_inclusive`, are set from the clause text: BCEA s37(1)(a) "six months or less" and SD1 clause 23(1)(a) "during the first four weeks" both put the boundary in the LOWER band; BCEA s37(1)(c)(i) "one year or more" puts that boundary in the UPPER band. `statutory/checks.py::check_notice_bands()` reconciles that exactly one of "the lower band's `service_to_inclusive`" and "the upper band's `service_from_inclusive`" is true at every shared boundary — both true is an overlap, both false is a gap, caught even when the boundary VALUES themselves touch exactly | ORIGINALLY (WRONG): every exact boundary was put in the upper band, on the reasoning that this matched `EffectiveDatedModel`'s own inclusive-start/exclusive-end convention and was the "safe" (never under-generous) direction. Both halves of that reasoning failed against the actual statutes, not against a hypothetical: BCEA s37(1)(a) states "six months OR LESS" and SD1 clause 23(1)(a) states "DURING THE FIRST FOUR WEEKS" - both name the boundary as belonging to the LOWER band, in so many words, and reading them the other way gave a resigning contract cleaner one working day's notice where the Act requires four weeks (correct) but ALSO gave a resigning BCEA/domestic employee two or four weeks where the Act requires one (WRONG, and wrong in the dangerous direction): SD1 clause 23(1)(c) forbids requiring an employee to give longer notice than the employer must, so notice is SYMMETRIC and an over-stated period does not merely cost the employer more, it holds a RESIGNING EMPLOYEE in employment longer than the law permits - "more notice is safer" is true only for the employer's own obligation, never for the employee's, and the employee carries the wrong direction of this error. Caught by three failing tests written before the fix, one per sector, each reproducing the exact wrong answer at the exact boundary the Act names. The one boundary where an upper-band reading IS correct - BCEA's one year mark, where (b) and (c)(i) genuinely overlap in the Act's own text - is now expressed as DATA (`service_from_inclusive=True` on the one-year band) rather than as a rule applied everywhere, and is flagged for the labour law review (O-06) as the genuine judgement call it is, distinct from the other two boundaries which were never actually judgement calls at all. |

| D-181 | 15 Sep 2026 | Leave (P6 chunk 4) | BCEA s22's sick leave accrual, implemented in `leave/accrual.py::_accrue_sick()`, never through `AccrualMethod`/`accrual_method_for()` — s22 is prescribed, not agreement-based. Cycle 1 (anchored to engagement start, D-B) runs an attendance-driven ratio phase — one day per `sick_leave_first_six_months_ratio` days worked (`attendance_day.days_worked_equivalent`), monthly and idempotent like ANNUAL's own per-days-worked method — until the `SICK_LEAVE_FIRST_PERIOD_MONTHS` mark (a new `statutory_parameter`, `reference/ref-2026.03.01-sick-accrual.json`, the SIX itself being the exact gap chunk 1's own docstring flagged and never claimed to close). **The transition is ONE top-up transaction of `entitlement_quantity - accrued_so_far`, not a reset and not an addition of the remaining ratio.** Proof: let A = accrued during the ratio phase, T = taken during it (both already in the ledger, so the pre-transition balance is A - T); s22(2)'s own words entitle the employee to the full six-week-equivalent E LESS T. Posting `E - A` brings the balance to `(A - T) + (E - A) = E - T` — exactly the Act's own figure — with T cancelling out of the top-up's own arithmetic entirely, so it is correct regardless of how much was taken. This is the ONLY reading that satisfies both of task 2's named failure modes at once: doubling the entitlement (adding `E` outright) would ignore what was already accrued, and stranding what was taken (topping up by `E - A - T` or similar) double-counts T, which the ledger has already applied once. Cycle 2 onward can never fall inside the first six months of employment (cycle 1 alone runs the full 36-month cycle by construction), so it skips the ratio phase entirely and is granted the full six-week-equivalent upfront, once, at cycle start — the same shape as ANNUAL's `UPFRONT_ANNUAL` method, but reached by cycle number rather than an agreement AMENDED 16 Sep 2026 (P6 chunk 4b) — THE ARITHMETIC IS UNCHANGED; THE REASONING ABOVE WAS WRONG. This row justified E − A as an employer protection. It is not one. The settled reading: s22(3)'s ratio is a RESTRICTION ON AVAILABILITY WITHIN THE ONE CYCLE, not a second, separate entitlement. There is one entitlement per cycle, E (s22(2)); during the first six months only the accrued ratio amount of it may be drawn; at six months the rest of E becomes available; days already drawn are days already drawn. E less what was taken is therefore not the employer reducing anything — it is the balance of a single entitlement. The two readings diverge the moment anyone asks whether an employee who took four days in month three is entitled to 30 more or 26 more: under "second entitlement" 30, and the employer is over-charged; under "one entitlement, restricted availability" 26. It also explains why s22(4) is drafted as a permission rather than a rule: Parliament permits the employer to decline to treat the first six months' days as a free extra; it does not create a discretion to deduct twice. Because s22(4) says MAY, the deduction is now an employer election rather than hard-coded — see D-186. The default election reproduces this row's arithmetic exactly, and `test_sick_leave_transition_at_six_months_grants_the_full_entitlement_less_taken` passed unchanged across the change. | Asserted as the TOTAL balance after transition, not the top-up transaction's own delta, in `test_sick_leave_transition_at_six_months_grants_the_full_entitlement_less_taken` — a delta-only assertion could not distinguish this reading from either of the two the brief named as wrong. `EntitlementNotResolvableError` (chunk 1) still applies unchanged: an employee with no captured work schedule cannot have a SICK cycle's weeks-to-days entitlement computed at all, sick leave included. **CONFIRMED AGAINST THE ACT TEXT BY KOBUS, 20 September 2026**, during the verification pass, reading s22(1)-(4) rather than a summary of them. The cycle runs from COMMENCEMENT OF EMPLOYMENT (s22(1)(a)), and s22(3)'s one-day-per-26 is an INTERIM AVAILABILITY CALCULATION that makes sick leave available pro rata to a new employee — not a second entitlement. The first cycle therefore resolves to E − A. **The model was verified to match the reading rather than assumed to**: the ratio accruals and the top-up post to ONE cycle carrying ONE `entitlement_quantity`, and `sick_first_cycle_top_up()` returns `entitlement - accrued`, which is only coherent if the ratio amount was already part of E. `test_s22_3_restricts_availability_within_one_entitlement_not_a_second_accrual` pins it on the SUM OF ACCRUALS over the cycle rather than on the balance — the two readings agree in the ordinary case and diverge the moment anything else changes, which is the worst kind of agreement, and the accrual total is what says which one is implemented. Watched rejecting the second-accrual shape before being kept. **Kobus also observes that s22(4)'s "may" sits awkwardly against a cycle that begins at commencement**, and would read better as "must": if s22(3) only ever restricted availability within the one entitlement, there is nothing for an employer to elect. He is right about the arithmetic. **THE CODE MUST NOT FORCE IT, AND THIS IS WHY — do not "fix" this by removing the setting.** The BCEA is a FLOOR. An employer who declines the reduction gives MORE paid sick leave over the cycle than the Act requires, which is lawful, may be required by the contract of employment, and is a position a payroll system must be able to record. Hard-coding the reduction would make a lawful and more generous arrangement unrepresentable, and the employer already honouring it would have to be talked out of it by their software — the identical argument D-242 makes for BCCCI clause 4.5(g)'s three minimums. `SICK_FIRST_CYCLE_REDUCTION` therefore stays, defaulting TRUE, and its copy now says plainly that the default IS the statutory position and the alternative exceeds it rather than offering two equal options (D-186 amended). Whether the "may" bears the reading above is a CONFIRMATION on O-06, not a blocker: the engineering is settled either way. |

A SUPERSEDED version is excluded from both: it is kept forever as the audit record (D-199) and nothing resolves against it, so demanding its verification would hold the gate shut permanently - watched not firing in its own test. **The lesson is the one this codebase keeps relearning**: a guard written against one shape of data keeps reading correctly after the data changes shape, and says nothing at all. |
| D-251 | 20 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Tooling (P2 close-out) | **The verification workbook is a WORK AID and never a source of truth, and `importverification` writes the verification record and nothing else.** `manage.py exportverification` writes every loaded figure to an .xlsx grouped by SOURCE DOCUMENT, then clause, then effective date; `manage.py importverification` reads the ticks back and calls the existing `verifystatutory` path for each version whose figures are all checked. A workbook value that differs from the loaded value REFUSES that version and names the row, both values, and says the command never writes a figure. | **The grouping is the whole design.** A person verifies by opening GN R.7083 once and checking every figure that cites it; grouped by table the same gazette is opened once per table that cites it and the job never finishes — which is why it had not been started. Sorting by anything else is the thing to fix. **The refusal is the other half.** An importer that could correct a figure would be a second write path into reference data with no citation, no checksum and no second reader — every guard in `statutory/loader.py` routed around by a spreadsheet at 23:00. So a disagreement between the workbook and the database is a QUERY for a human and the version stays unverified: if the workbook is right, the gazette was transcribed wrongly and that is a new load, not a tick. The round trip is tested both ways — export, tick everything, import, and assert no figure moved; then the same with one cell edited, and assert it refuses and names the row. Three further refusals are watched firing: a QUERY with no note, the loader verifying their own load, and a half-finished workbook verifying only what is complete. **The formatter is shared in both directions** — the workbook renders R 32,40 and the import regenerates the same string to compare, so there is one notion of what a figure looks like rather than two that drift. |

Nothing was broken by that until something had to ask the question, and every consumer so far asked "what applies on this date", which is answered by the effective dates alone. **Recovering it from the fixtures is right rather than merely expedient**: `checkstatutory` already reconciles every version's checksum against its file, so the fixtures are known to match the database, and a fixture is the only place that says "these rows arrived together as one load". A foreign key on every cited table would be the alternative and is a large migration for a question only this tooling asks. **The cost is recorded rather than hidden**: a row present in the database but in no fixture reports as "(not in any fixture)" instead of being guessed at, and a fixture naming a sector this database does not hold is skipped, because its rows cannot be here either. |

| D-256 | 20 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Schema (P2) | **A verification tick is evidence, and evidence does not live in an unmergeable binary.** `reference_figure_check` records one row per checked figure - the row key, the version it belonged to at the time, the outcome (checked or queried), the value as it read, who checked it, the date they say they did it, and the note. Append-only by trigger; a change of mind inserts a second row and the first one stands. `exportverification` pre-fills every recorded tick, so a re-export after loading new reference rows carries the old work forward and the new figures arrive blank, and `importverification` writes the records. A version verifies when the DATABASE says every figure in it is checked, not when one workbook happens to hold them all. | **The workbook shipped with two hazards and this closes the one that bites first.** Git cannot merge an .xlsx, and `--force` destroyed every tick because the ticks existed only in the file - with three P2 items still to load (source code 3901, the bank account lengths, the predecessor BCCCI agreement), each needing a re-export, the first one would have thrown away however many evenings had accumulated. **TenantOptionalModel, deliberately.** This is not employer or employee data and no tenant may see it: it records which member of Labourmax staff read which gazette. `TenantSharedModel`'s NULL means the opposite - *available to all* - and would put the platform's own audit trail on every subscriber's screen; CLAUDE.md's test for the shared base is whether a row would be safe on a competitor's screen, and an operations record naming staff and their queries is not a catalogue. `TenantScopedModel` cannot hold it at all, there being no tenant to attribute it to. The third access rule is what makes the commands work without `platform_context()`: a session with no tenant pinned - which is every management command - reads and writes exactly the NULL-tenant rows. **The summary sheet is computed from the database, not with a COUNTIFS**, because openpyxl writes a formula and only Excel evaluates it, so a freshly written file would read as zero everywhere until somebody opened it - and zero is precisely the wrong answer to "how far have I got". That is the test of whether the merge worked and it is asserted on an unopened file. **A refused version still records its sound ticks**: discarding a hundred good ones over one edited cell is the same data loss in a smaller wrapper. A row whose value has drifted from the database is NOT recorded, because it was not checked against what is loaded, whatever the cell says. The order-independence of two partial workbooks is tested by parametrising the import order and asserting the SAME final state from both, which is what makes it a test of order-independence rather than two tests of one order each. |

Six versions superseded, no figure changed; the four BCCCI ones were machine-verified and needed `--supersede-verified`, so they are unverified again — correct, because the citations a verifier reads have changed. **The guard warns and never refuses**, because a genuine revision of a guide is two documents and a real case. | D-258 | 20 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Tooling (P2) | **The verification workbook asks one question per LOOKUP, not one per database figure.** A check group is the figures sharing a source document, a clause, an effective date and a database row, capped at 8; the row shows every figure it covers inline, and ticking it records a `ReferenceFigureCheck` for each of them. 582 figures become 129 questions. A tick over figures the person never looked at is the one thing a group must not be. **The cap is 8** — a source code is six and renders legibly on one line, so six is the benchmark and eight the headroom; above it the inline summary wraps to three lines and the tick stops meaning "I read all of these", which is the only thing making a group safe. One sector, two instruments (D-240) is now a settled fact of this schema, and every resolver that takes a sector and not an area is suspect for the same reason; this one was found by walking the 1 April 2026 boundary figure by figure rather than by reading the code. The proving test uses deliberately DIFFERENT percentages, because the real ones cannot tell the two answers apart. |
| D-267 | 20 Sep 2026 | Leave / Statutory data (P6) | **The long-service annual leave band is read by the engine that spends it, and a cycle's SERVICE is measured on its last day while its INSTRUMENT is chosen on its first.** `leave/cycles.py::_statutory_entitlement_quantity()` now calls `resolve.annual_leave_days()` and passes the employee's wage area; `resolve.annual_leave_days()` gains `service_on_date`, defaulting to `on_date` so every existing caller is unchanged. Where the leave type's cycle length and the gazetted one disagree, it REFUSES. | **Two defects, and the second was the worse one.** D-243 added four `leave_rule_set` columns and the resolver for BCCCI clause 9.1(b) and nothing read them — `leave/cycles.py` went on reading `annual_leave_days_per_cycle_*` directly, so a KwaZulu-Natal cleaner of twelve years' standing had 18 days written against them by the only code that decides how much leave they get. And it passed no `sector_area`, so that same employee resolved Sectoral Determination 1's rule set rather than the agreement binding them — D-266's shape, here costing leave days rather than being right by coincidence. The area is assembled exactly as `employees/remuneration.py` already assembles it for the minimum wage, and deliberately in the same order: an employee whose leave and whose wage floor came from different instruments would be worse than either one being wrong. **THE MID-CYCLE CROSSING, AND WHY IT IS NOT THE QUESTION IT LOOKS LIKE.** The first design measured service when the cycle opened, on the reasoning that a cycle's entitlement is written once and never recomputed. That is right about the mechanism and wrong about the answer: a twelve-month cycle anchored to the engagement start (D-163) puts the tenth anniversary ON a cycle's FIRST day, and "more than ten years" excludes that day — so the band would arrive a full cycle late, at eleven years of service rather than ten years and a day, for every employee who ever reaches it. The crossing is not a rare edge; it is where the boundary always falls. **The reading loaded**: the entitlement is stated "in respect of each annual leave cycle" and accrues across it, so the service that earns it is the service the employee has when that cycle completes. The instrument is still read as at the cycle's first day, because a gazette published part-way through must not retroactively move a figure already accrued against (D-107). Two questions, two dates, and the function now takes both rather than conflating them. **The alternative reading is named rather than hidden** (O-06): measuring at the opening day is defensible if one reads the band as a status the employee must already hold when the cycle begins. It was rejected because it denies a year of the entitlement to everybody, and because nothing in clause 9.1 says the status is tested at the start. **What refuses**: `leave_type.cycle_months` is what generates cycles and `annual_leave_cycle_months` is what the instrument states, and while they disagree the boundary is tested on a date the instrument does not mean — so the entitlement is not resolved at all rather than written wrong into `leave_cycle.entitlement_quantity`, which every later accrual, application and termination payout reads. **The property test is the real guard**: over every service length from one to twenty years, each generated cycle's entitlement equals the resolver computed independently at that cycle's own last day, the figure is always one of the two and never a blend, and once a cycle carries the band no later cycle drops back — an employee cannot serve longer and earn less leave. That last invariant is what an off-by-one in the boundary breaks, in exactly one cycle out of a working life, which is why a property finds it and a worked example does not. |
| D-268 | 20 Sep 2026 | Leave / Statutory data (P2, P6) | **The three KwaZulu-Natal leave types are loaded: study leave, shop steward leave and the prenatal clinic day.** Two new `leave_type` system rows (PRENATAL, SHOP_STEWARD) plus STUDY, which was already seeded; four new `statutory_parameter` figures scoped to contract cleaning Area B, in their own version for each agreement; and three resolvers, because a figure nothing reads is the D-243 defect. **None of the three accrues.** | **Read off BOTH gazettes clause by clause, and all three are word for word identical between the two agreements, figures included** — asserted by a test, not assumed (D-265's rule). Study leave is clause 12.1 (predecessor 11.1): one day to PREPARE for and one day to WRITE each examination, on full pay, to any employee other than a casual, on satisfactory proof that they were allowed to write and have duly written. Shop steward leave is clause 20.4(a) (predecessor 19.4(a)): 4 days a year for an office bearer of a representative trade union, 6 for any other shop steward. The prenatal clinic day is clause 13.2 (predecessor 12.2): one fully paid day in EACH of the 3 months before the expected date of confinement, on proof of attendance. **NONE OF THEM IS A PER-CYCLE BANK, and that is the whole shape of this decision.** Study leave is per EXAMINATION, the prenatal day is per PREGNANCY, and shop steward leave is per year but at one of two figures. So each is a `statutory_parameter` rather than a `leave_rule_set` column, and all three catalogue rows carry `accrues=False` — `accrue_employee()` returns early on that, so nothing is ever granted automatically. PRENATAL and SHOP_STEWARD carry `balance_source=none` for the same reason: an annual bank would be an invented shape, and `ensure_cycles()` would write a zero entitlement against it and mark every day of such leave unpaid. **Which shop steward figure applies is a fact about the PERSON** — whether they are an office bearer of a representative trade union — and no column on `employee` carries it, so `resolve.shop_steward_leave()` takes the status as an argument rather than guessing, the same shape as `works_over_27_hours_week` (D-110). **The office bearer gets FEWER days than an ordinary shop steward**, which reads like the two limbs were swapped in drafting; both agreements print it that way three years apart, so it is transcribed as printed and flagged rather than corrected (O-32's rule), and a test asserts the direction by name so nobody "fixes" it later. **The prenatal figures were already loaded and read by nothing** (D-244), which is the same gap D-243 left behind with the long-service band and was found here only because this chunk seeded the leave type that spends them — the third instance, and the reason every figure in this chunk arrived with a resolver in the same commit. **What is NOT built**: nothing enforces the three-day prenatal cap or the 4/6-day shop steward cap. The resolvers are the only place those figures live and the application layer does not read them (O-31). That is a smaller gap than it was — before this, the figures had no reader at all and two of the three types did not exist. |
| D-269 | 20 Sep 2026 | Leave / Schema (P6) | **The prenatal and shop steward caps are enforced, and NEITHER REFUSES.** A day over the ceiling is recorded as unpaid through the same columns every other unpaid reason uses (D-188), never a third mechanism and never a refusal. Two declared facts were needed: `employee_union_role` (new, effective-dated, tenant-scoped) and `leave_application.expected_date_of_confinement`. The cap decides PAY, never whether the leave may be taken — D-174's rule for an overdrawn application, restated for a ceiling that comes from the instrument rather than from a ledger, which is exactly how parental leave already works (D-201). The one thing that IS refused is a prenatal application with no due date, because without it there is no window and the cap could not bite at all. Both caps run BEFORE the overdraw arithmetic, so neither type reports an overdraw against the zero balance it does not have. **A TEST CHANGED THE DESIGN, and this is the part worth reading.** The prenatal windows were first implemented as one-month periods counted back from the declared due date — the reading "the 3 months prior to" most naturally carries, and it always yields exactly three periods. Those windows MOVE. A due date revised by ten days shifts every boundary, so a calendar month already paid for can fall outside the new window and a second paid day appears in it. A revised due date is an ordinary event — that is what "expected" means — and it must not buy a fourth paid day. The windows are therefore the three CALENDAR months before the month of confinement, which do not move: "one paid day per calendar month" is then checkable without knowing which pregnancy a day belongs to, and the three-day total falls out of it instead of needing a counter of its own. Both readings give exactly three days, so nothing was lost by taking the one that cannot be walked. **The shop steward year is the calendar year**, because clause 4.5(d) is the only place this agreement defines a year and it says "Calendar Year" in terms. Flagged for the labour law review (O-06) rather than asserted: an employment-anniversary year is the other defensible reading and would move which days are paid. **`employee_union_role` is a TABLE and not a boolean** because the cap on one application depends on a status held over a PERIOD — declared per application, two applications in one year could state two statuses and there would be no answer to how many days the person gets. Invariant 2 settles it. It is an ENUM and not a boolean because the fact is tri-state, and NO ROW MEANS NO ENTITLEMENT, the safe direction: an employee nobody recorded as a shop steward is capped at nothing rather than at six days. **Not in sheet 02** — the workbook's `leave_type.code` list stops at UNPAID and knows nothing of this entitlement, the same silence `employee_import_batch` was built into (D-144). RLS went in the CREATING migration, and the generated isolation suite picked it up the same day (D-169's lesson). **The figures are not on it**: the 4 and the 6 stay in `statutory_parameter` with their citation and the row only says which applies — D-110 exactly. **`expected_date_of_confinement` carries no CHECK tying it to PRENATAL, and that is a limit rather than an oversight**: a CHECK cannot read through the `leave_type` foreign key, and this table freezes no `leave_type_code` beside it the way `payslip_line` freezes `component_code` (invariant 7). The parental declaration lives with the identical limit. So the service refuses by name and a test watches it refuse. **Found while writing the tests**: the first version of every assertion queried `application.days` with no tenant pinned, so RLS returned nothing and every "this day is unpaid" test passed for entirely the wrong reason — the count-reads-as-none trap, from a table this codebase has had in CLAUDE.md since P0. |
| D-270 | 20 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Verification (P2) | **A reference version may be verified by more than one person, and `verified_by_users` records them all.** The one-checker refusal in `importverification` is lifted; `verifystatutory` takes `--also-checked-by`; `verified_by_user` stays as the verifier of RECORD. Two rules widened with it: the second-pair-of-eyes check now runs over EVERY checker, and a version is machine-verified if ANY checker is a development identity. Separately, `--dry-run` is now apply-and-roll-back. | **The refusal was only ever about attribution, and said so** — "verifystatutory records one verifier, so agree who signs". It protected nothing. **The workbook's own shape guaranteed it would fire**: it is organised by SOURCE DOCUMENT because that is how a person verifies, one gazette in one sitting (D-251), and versions cut ACROSS documents — REF-2026.03.01-r2 alone carries 140 figures over several. So sharing the pass out by document, the obvious way for two people to split it, guaranteed a refusal on every version spanning both their documents. And two people checking different figures is a STRONGER result than one person checking all of them, not a weaker one. **The module docstring had claimed this worked** — "two people working two halves on two evenings complete it between them" — which was true of accumulation ACROSS workbooks and false for one version; the prose and the code disagreed and the prose was the one nobody ran. **`verified_by_user` is kept** rather than replaced: a CHECK pairs it with `verified_at` so a version cannot be verified with nobody's name on it, and a CHECK cannot reach a many-to-many. It is always one of the checkers, pinned by test. **IT WAS COVERING ONE REAL RULE BY ACCIDENT**, which is the finding worth keeping: the loader-cannot-verify check ran against `checkers[0]` only, so lifting the refusal on its own would have let a loader verify their own load simply by being the second name on the version. It now runs over every checker in both commands, and the test asserts the refusal NAMES the loader rather than merely raising — without that assertion it would have passed on the old multi-checker refusal and proved nothing. **D-262 needed the same widening**: with several checkers, reading only the signer would let half a version checked by nobody ride in on the half a person did check, so ANY machine identity among them taints the version. `unusable_q()` reaches the other checkers through an EXISTS subquery rather than a join, because a multi-valued join inside `exclude()` is exactly where "exclude rows having any match" and "exclude rows where the joined row matches" come apart — and an EXISTS also cannot duplicate rows in the `filter()` direction the payroll gate uses. **`--dry-run` reported the DATABASE**, so a fully ticked workbook always said "0 of N checked": it read as a failure and sent a person looking for a bug that was not there. It now runs the real code and rolls the transaction back — D-145's "preview IS apply, rolled back", for the same reason: a second reporting path would have to be kept in step with the first and would drift. **The two changes landed in one commit deliberately**, against the one-concern rule: the rollback is what makes the now-unconditional `verifystatutory` call safe, so splitting them would leave an intermediate commit in which a dry run writes to the database. **Also corrected**: the closing error said "Nothing about them was recorded" about refused versions, which was the opposite of the truth — every sound tick is recorded precisely so one bad cell cannot cost a hundred good ticks (D-256). |
| D-271 | 20 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Checks (P2) | **A SUPERSEDED version's development verifier is a fact about history, not a standing warning.** `check_machine_verified_versions()` now returns live versions only; `superseded_machine_verified()` returns the labels of the rest, and `checkstatutory` prints them as ONE note line rather than as Issues. The live check is otherwise unchanged. | **Four of them warned on every run, and would have forever** — the message said so itself, "(superseded, so nothing reads it)". `in_force_on()` cannot see a superseded version and no payroll run can reach one; it survives solely as the audit record (D-199), and nothing was ever computed against it because a machine-verified version was never usable in the first place (D-262). So there is no action behind the warning: re-verifying a row nothing reads is make-work. **A command that never returns clean trains people to skim its output, which is how a real finding gets missed** — that is the defect, and it is a defect in the REPORTING rather than in the rule. `checkstatutory` against this database now says "Every check reconciles" with one note, where it printed four warnings before. **Not an Issue, deliberately.** `Issue` carries only `blocking`, so anything returned from a check renders as `[REFUSED]` or `[warning]`; a third severity would have meant changing the shape every check shares. The note belongs to the COMMAND, which is the thing that reports, and `run_all()` stays a list of findings — which also keeps it out of `verifystatutory`'s gate, where it never had any business. **Not dropped in silence either.** A check that went quiet could not be told apart from one that had stopped working, so the count and the labels stay on screen. **The membership test is the safe direction round**: a version counts as superseded only when another row positively claims to supersede it, so a bug in that query costs four warnings too many rather than filing a LIVE finding under history — the failure that would matter. (`superseded_by` is the reverse side of a OneToOne, with no `_id` attribute and an exception rather than None on an un-superseded row, which is how this area produced a silent wrong answer once already.) |
| D-272 | 21 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Verification (P2) | **The import round trip works and can no longer be silent.** A workbook with seventeen marked rows recorded NOTHING and said nothing. `_sift()` now resolves the checker and the date rather than only checking the cells are non-blank, the count is printed unconditionally, a STALE workbook is refused, and `--force` refuses over a file holding marked rows absent from the database. | **THE CAUSE.** `_sift()` validated that 'checked by' and the date were NOT BLANK and nothing more. `_record()` then resolved them — an `AppUser` lookup and a date parse — and on failure did a bare `continue`, under a comment claiming the row was "named in _sift's problems". It never was. Seventeen rows ticked by an address with no account: nothing written. And the count printed only `if recorded`, so nil printed nothing at all and the run read as success. Two silent skips and one silent total. **The verifier having no login is the ORDINARY case**, not a freak one — the email a person types is their own. **The fix puts both resolutions in `_sift()`**, where a failure becomes a problem that refuses the version and names the cell and the value; `_record()` now RAISES if either is still unresolved, because that would mean a defect in the sifting, and the one thing that loop must never do again is pass over a marked row quietly. The count is unconditional — "2 marked row(s) read, 0 recorded" — and a run that reads marked rows and writes nothing says why, so a correct no-op is distinguishable from a discard. **STALE WORKBOOKS.** A workbook is a SNAPSHOT of the check groups, and the Summary sheet now carries a hash of their keys. Load a fixture afterwards and what the file does NOT mention stops meaning "nothing to do" and starts meaning "rows this file never knew about" — a version could be reported complete with figures nobody had seen. Keyed on group keys alone, so recording ticks never invalidates a workbook and the pass can run over as many evenings as it takes. **`--force`** is for a file whose work is already in the database; over un-imported ticks it is precisely the destructive operation D-256 exists to prevent, and it now refuses and names the rows. **The end-to-end test had never been run.** Each command was tested and the SEAM between them was not: export, tick, import, re-export is one test now — 2 marked, 4 figure-checks recorded, 2 rows carried forward. |
| D-273 | 21 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Verification (P2) | **The cited documents are assembled and each check links to its clause.** `manage.py fetchsources` downloads every distinct `source_url` into `reference/sources/` (gitignored); the workbook's CLAUSE cell opens the local PDF at the page the clause is printed on where that can be found confidently, at the document otherwise, and is plain text where neither is possible. | **The pass is 156 checks across 18 documents, and the reading is thirty seconds while finding the right page of the right PDF is two minutes.** That ratio is why it had not been started, and it is a tooling problem rather than a compliance one. **Keyed on the URL and not the document**: the BCEA cites THREE urls under one document string — the Act from labour.gov.za, the Act from gov.za, and Form BCEA1A, the Summary of the Act. Taking the first would have linked the two rows citing the SUMMARY at the Act itself, and a verifier would have hunted for the summary's wording in a document that does not contain it. A wrong document is worse than no link. **A WRONG PAGE IS WORSE THAN NO PAGE**, for the same reason, so `statutory/sourcepages.py` is biased throughout towards answering nothing. Two rules earned their place by failing first: the number must head a line AND be followed by a letter, because A LINE HOLDING ONLY A NUMBER IS A PAGE NUMBER — without that, "35" matched thirty-three of the Act's forty pages and the answer was whichever page happened to be numbered 35; and a contents page is detected by HEADING DENSITY rather than by its heading, because an arrangement of sections runs over several pages and only the first says so, which had s23 and s43 both answered with a contents page. **NOTHING IS EXTRACTED, QUOTED OR SUMMARISED.** This is navigation only: supplying the clause text would make the pass Claude Code's transcription checked against Claude Code's transcription, which is the one thing it exists to avoid. **The PDFs are not committed** — tens of megabytes of public documents, and the URL in `source_reference` IS the citation; the file is a convenience `fetchsources` rebuilds. **Coverage is honest and low**: 5 of 156 groups open at a page, 73 more at the document, 78 at nothing. The limits are in the DATA rather than the matcher — see O-38. |

| D-277 | 22 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Notice (P2) | **BCCCI clause 21.1(b) is not a contradiction and D-241 was wrong to load it as one.** The two-week limb is the general rule and the one-week limb is an exception for employees on probation, so the four-weeks-to-six-months window is TWO bands, not one refusal: one week on probation, two weeks off it. `probation_condition` is data on the band, `check_notice_bands()` reconciles each lane separately, and `resolve.notice_band()` REFUSES if the caller does not say which employee this is. A general rule and an exception stated for a named class govern different people; reading them as rivals is what made the window look irresolvable. **THE CONDITION IS DATA, the same way inclusivity is** (D-158's own lesson): a `TextChoices` with a CHECK, defaulted to `any` because an instrument that says nothing about probation really does apply its band to everybody — the opposite of D-198's no-default, and for the opposite reason. What a forgetful author can still get wrong is stating one lane and not the other, and that is caught as a GAP rather than left to the column: `check_notice_bands()` now reconciles the unconditional bands plus the on-probation ones, then the unconditional bands plus the off-probation ones, and each lane must start at zero and tile with no gap or overlap. **`sequence` stopped meaning service order** the moment two bands could cover one range, so the check sorts by each band's own range and the new off-probation band is sequence 4 rather than renumbering two loaded rows to make room. **RESOLVING A CONTESTED BAND IS A THIRD KIND OF LOAD.** A corrected READING had nowhere to land: `--supersede` refused it as a figure change and an ordinary load refused it as an edit, so the only way in was deleting rows. A contested band is the one row in this schema that explicitly holds NO figure — `notice_value` is NULL and `is_contested` says why — so filling it in moves nothing, which is the whole of what "a statutory value is never edited" protects. The loader now permits exactly that, plus the conditional twin that comes with it, and refuses everything else including a twin with no resolution to belong to. The guard that matters is the one already there: a VERIFIED version still refuses to be superseded without `--supersede-verified`, because somebody who ticked a contested row ticked "this really is irreconcilable". **`probation_end_date` already existed and NOTHING HAD EVER READ IT** — P4 shipped it in `employees/0002` and it has sat unread since. A column nobody reads is a column nobody fills in, which was harmless until it became the fact that chooses between one week's notice and two. `employees/probation.py` reads it, and states the boundary rather than assuming it: the last day of probation is a day ON probation, the way `termination_date` is the last day of service. No date means NOT on probation — inferring one from a blank field would hand an employee the shorter notice on the strength of an empty cell. **Clause 3's four-month cap is loaded and enforced at ENGAGEMENT**, not merely read at termination: a probation term longer than the agreement permits is a breach on the day it is captured. Loaded as `PROBATION_MAX_MONTHS` scoped to the sector and area, two rows, one per agreement — and **NOT re-read from the gazette in this pass**, because both BCCCI gazettes extract as unreadable text (O-38); it is carried from D-247's page-by-page reading, where it already stood in the notice bands' own contested_reason, and its workbook rows say so. **PAYMENT IN LIEU needs no figure from the agreement.** Clause 21.2(a) prices only the one working day and the two weeks, which D-241 counted as a second reason the window could not be read. BCEA s38(1), read off the Act itself (GG 18491, 5 December 1997, p19): "Instead of giving an employee notice in terms of section 37, an employer may pay the employee the remuneration the employee would have received, calculated in accordance with section 35, if the employee had worked during the notice period." It prices whatever the period is. Two soft spots go to O-06 rather than being asserted: s38 says "in terms of section 37" and this notice is in terms of an extended collective agreement, and clause 21.2(a)(ii) prices the two weeks on the WAGE while s38 pays s35 REMUNERATION, which is more for an employee with allowances. |
| D-278 | 22 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Verification (P2) | **A reference version now records when it STOPS applying, derived at load.** `applies_from` alone made every version that ever applied applicable forever, so the payroll gate demanded verification of four 2023 BCCCI versions no 2026 run can read a figure from, and the workbook reported two superseded versions as "0 of 0 checked". Both now say what they are instead. | Two reports were telling a person to do work that could not be done. The gate's refusal named the 2023 agreement among the versions somebody must verify before anyone is paid — but every row it loaded closes on 1 April 2026, so nothing after that date reaches it. The workbook's "Still outstanding" list carried `REF-2026.03.01-NOTICE-BANDS-3` and `REF-2026.03.01-RULES-r4` at "0 of 0 checked", which is not a version somebody is behind on: they were SUPERSEDED the day before, their fixtures rewritten under the new labels, so no row maps back and they own nothing to check. A line reading "0 of 0" looks like a broken report and sent a person looking for figures that were never there. **This is D-271's lesson one command along**: a list that never empties, or that holds things nobody can act on, is a list people learn to skim — which is how a real finding gets missed. **`applies_until` is DERIVED, never declared.** An author asked to state it would be stating twice what the rows already say, and the second statement is the one that goes stale; the loader takes the latest `effective_to` across everything the fixture loaded and leaves it NULL the moment one row is open-ended. Open-ended is the SAFE direction: a version wrongly thought closed drops out of the gate, and the gate is the only thing between unverified figures and a payslip. Eighteen versions predate the column, so `manage.py rescanversionscope` replays the shipped fixtures to recover it — the same replay `check_fixture_checksums()` and `verification.row_versions()` already do, because no cited table carries a version foreign key (D-252). `importverification` now separates "Out of scope — not checked" from "Still outstanding" and says which of the two reasons applies to each. Nothing is deleted and nothing is hidden. |
| D-279 | 23 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Citations (P2) | **A citation must contain every figure its row carries, and SD1's leave row's did not.** It named clauses 18, 19 and 22 — annual, sick, family responsibility — for a row that also carries two MATERNITY figures, which are clause 20. It is the same shape as D-124 and D-257 and it is the third citation defect this pass has produced, which is the argument for the pass. **THE NUMBERING IS CONFIRMED, from two texts a year and a half apart.** The 1999 determination (Kobus's copy, lawlibrary.org.za) reads 18(6) annual leave paid out on termination, 20(2) maternity may commence four weeks before the expected birth, 20(3) no work for six weeks after it, 22(1) the two eligibility limbs, 22(2) three days' family responsibility leave. D-194 independently read clause 22(1) off the consolidation as at 1 March 2026 and off the 1999 notice and found them word for word. So 18/19/20/22 holds in both, and the pinpoint now names each with its subject — the workbook prints one row for a group of figures, and "clauses 18, 19 and 22" made the reader guess which clause answered which figure. **The document half of the citation is untouched, deliberately** (D-257): the workbook groups by it, so moving one character would split SD1 into two source documents and let a version read as incomplete after being fully checked. Pinned by a test. **THE FIGURES ARE PINNED IN A TEST**, spelled out rather than compared against anything that could move with them, because "this was a citation fix" is a claim that has to survive the next person: `statutory/tests/test_sd1_citation.py`. The loader's own supersede guard refuses a changed figure, and did — one citation re-encoded, nothing else — but that guard only fires on the day somebody reloads. **WHAT SD1 IS STILL NOT NAMED.** Task 1 asked whether "current consolidated text" is itself wrong because only the 1999 text was ever read. It is not: D-194 records reading a consolidation headed "(1 March 2026 - to date)". But that header also states the instrument — **"Government Notice 622 in Government Gazette 20064 dated 14 May 1999"** — which is a FOURTH and more precise identity than the three D-275 weighed, and it agrees with two of them on the date. The repo holds no SD1 text on disk and `fetchsources` downloads nothing for it, so this was not changed in this pass; it is the best candidate yet for closing the SD1 half of O-38 and is recorded here rather than acted on. **TWO OTHER SD1 ROWS ARE UNVERIFIED RATHER THAN WRONG**, listed and not touched. The termination row cites "clauses 3(3) and 24": the builder and the fixture note attribute the 4,333-week December bonus to clause 3(3), and O-25 attributes it to clause 24 — **the repo contradicts itself about which clause the bonus is**, and nothing anywhere records reading a severance clause at all, so `severance_weeks_per_completed_year` and `severance_requires_operational_reason` rest on an unrecorded attribution. (SD1's clause 3 is headed Remuneration, on the evidence of the unused `SD1_URL` `.../sd1_nr622_3__remuneration.php`, which favours the fixture over O-25.) The working time row cites a RANGE, "clauses 3(2), 8 to 17": the three attributions the repo records — 3(2) the six-hour minimum, 9 overtime, 16 the night allowance — all fall inside it, but nothing records which clauses carry the Sunday multipliers, the public holiday rules, the meal interval or the rest periods, so the range is plausible and unproven. Neither can be settled without the text, and there is none in the repo. **THE WAGES ARE CLEAN**: contract cleaning rates cite GN R.7083 and the BCCCI agreement, never SD1, so no SD1 wage row exists to be wrong. |
| D-280 | 24 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Public holidays (P2) | **Public Holidays Act s2(1) ADDS a day; it does not move one, and this build read it as a move.** A Sunday holiday is TWO public holidays - the Sunday, from Schedule 1, and the Monday s2(1) adds - and the generator emitted one row, dated the Monday. So 9 August 2026, 21 March 2027 and 26 December 2027 were not public holidays at all. Loaded as `REF-2026.03.01-HOLIDAYS` (4 rows), with the three stale notes corrected in `REF-2026.03.01-r4` (prose only, no figure moved). The 4 November 2026 election proclamation is loaded in the same version. | s2(1) reads: "The days mentioned in Schedule 1 shall be public holidays, and whenever any public holiday falls on a Sunday, the following Monday shall be a public holiday." It states two things and the second does not qualify the first - Schedule 1 fixes the date, 9 August IS National Women's Day, and nothing in s2(1) takes that away when the date lands on a Sunday. gov.za lists both days for both years. **The consequence was a live underpayment, not a cosmetic data point.** `resolve.is_public_holiday()` answered False on the Sunday, so an employee who worked it was priced under BCEA s16 and never reached s18, and a salaried employee who did not work it lost the s18(2)(a) paid day. Contract cleaning works Sundays routinely. **The guard did not exist, which is why nothing caught it.** `check_public_holiday_sundays()` now reconciles the pair in BOTH directions on every `checkstatutory` - a loaded Sunday must have its Monday, a Monday claiming s2(1) must have the Sunday that added it, `shifted_from_date` must BE a Sunday, and the holiday must be the day after it - blocking, because the failure is an underpayment on a named date. Watched refusing each shape, the shipped one reconstructed exactly, before being watched staying quiet on a correct pair. **TWO GUARDS MET OVER HOW TO LOAD IT.** `test_the_builder_still_reproduces_the_shipped_fixture` means correcting `public_holidays()` rewrites `ref-2026.03.01.json`, so its label goes to `-r4`; but `--supersede` is a re-encoding and may not ADD a row (D-199). The Sundays therefore arrive in their own ordinary load first, and `-r4` then carries only the three corrected notes. The three rows appear in both fixtures on purpose, and the loader reports them already present whichever order they run in. **A SECOND, OPPOSITE DEFECT CAME OUT OF THE FIRST.** `attendance/summary.py` counted a public holiday as "not worked" unless a day of type PUBLIC_HOLIDAY carried hours - safe only while a date could not also be something else. A contract cleaner working 9 August 2026 is naturally captured as SUNDAY, so the date would have read as a holiday nobody worked and paid the s18(2)(a) day ON TOP of the Sunday premium. It now reads hours actually worked, whatever bucket they landed in; a day of LEAVE still counts as not worked, because `days_worked_equivalent` is 1.000 for one and nobody was at work. **WHAT IS NOT DECIDED, deliberately (O-40):** `attendance_day.day_type` holds one value, and which of s16 and s18 prices a day that is both - or whether s18(2)(b)(ii)'s "the amount earned by the employee for the work performed on that day" means the SUNDAY amount and so stacks them - is a reading of the Act nothing here has made. `attendance/capture.py` WARNS on such a day, naming it, the same shape as D-157's night-hours warning; it re-buckets nothing and prices nothing. **THE ELECTION DAY WAS READ OFF THE GAZETTE.** Proclamation Notice 346 of 2026 is a scan with no text layer, so `fetchsources` and every extraction get the running header and stop; rendering page 3 as an image gives the operative wording, quoted in full in the fixture note so the workbook checks a transcription rather than inheriting one. `is_statutory=False` is its first use in the corpus, and **nothing anywhere reads that flag to decide holiday treatment** - `resolve.public_holiday_on()` does not filter on it and no caller consults it, so a s2A day is a public holiday for every BCEA purpose exactly as it should be. |
| D-281 | 24 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Verification (P2) | **Loading reference data while a verification pass is in flight strands the workbook on the desk, and the recovery is a KEYED MERGE rather than retyping.** `REF-2026.03.01-HOLIDAYS` added four check groups, which moved the corpus fingerprint and made a file holding 55 unimported ticks refuse to import at all. All 84 marks were carried across by `Group key` into a fresh export, nothing retyped and nothing lost; 309 checks recorded, 6 versions verified, none in force. | The staleness guard is right and fired exactly as D-272 designed it - a workbook is a SNAPSHOT of the groups that existed when it was exported, and importing an older one would report a version complete with figures in it nobody had seen. What the guard cannot know is that a particular change was purely ADDITIVE: the same 164 group keys plus 4 new ones, no key changed meaning, so every mark had exactly one destination. **That is what makes a merge safe here and it is not a general licence** - the test is that the old key set is a SUBSET of the new one, which is checkable in one line and was checked: removing the four new keys reproduced the workbook's own fingerprint to the character. **Copy only the four HUMAN columns** - Checked, Checked by, Date checked, Note - and leave the freshly exported "Every figure this tick covers" cell as the export wrote it, or the import's drift guard (D-251) compares a copy against a copy and stops being a guard. **The cheap fix is ordering, not tooling**: a load and a pass should not overlap, and where they must, import before loading. **AN "N" IS READ BY NOBODY AND BLOCKS THE EXPORT FOREVER** (O-42). The dropdown offers Y, N and QUERY and tells the reader "N: not yet", `IMPORTED_AS` maps only Y and QUERY, so an N is dropped in silence - and `exportverification` then refuses over the file as holding a marked row the database does not have, offering only "delete the file by hand". One N did exactly this here. Nothing is lost, because N means not-yet and carries no finding, but a person who used the dropdown as instructed lands on an export that will not run. |
| D-282 | 24 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Verification (P2) | **The Checked column's whole vocabulary is one list in `statutory/verification.py`, and `N` is gone from it.** The dropdown offered `Y,N,QUERY`, the importer recorded Y and QUERY only, and the export treated ANY non-blank cell as work it would not overwrite - so an `N`, which the dropdown itself defines as "not yet", was the one mark that made a workbook impossible to re-export. `RECORDED_AS` now feeds the dropdown, the `--force` guard and the importer alike; `CHECKED_TEXT` is derived from it rather than written beside it. | Closes O-42, raised the same day when one N in Kobus's file blocked the re-export and the only way out the message offered was "delete the file by hand". **The fault was not the guard, it was that three places each knew part of the vocabulary.** `exportverification.CHECKED_TEXT` and `importverification.IMPORTED_AS` were two dicts in two modules, each with a comment saying it was the inverse of the other - the shape this codebase has a name for (D-122, D-147: one spec, both sides, because a hand-maintained twin drifts). The dropdown was a third list, a string literal in a third place, and it was the one carrying the extra value. Nothing had to drift for this to break; it shipped wrong. **A mark is WORK only if the importer would record it**, so the guard now asks `recorded_outcome()` instead of asking whether the cell is non-blank, and a deferral costs nothing to overwrite because nothing was recorded for it. The D-272 refusal is untouched and pinned by two tests that pass against the old code and the new: an unimported Y and an unimported QUERY both still refuse. **The case bug travelled with it and is fixed in the same line.** The importer upper-cased the cell and the export did not, so the two disagreed about what a cell said - invisible for `y`, which both treated as work, and live for `n`, which the export called work and the importer called nothing. `recorded_outcome()` normalises once, for both. **Dropping an N is now said out loud** rather than quietly: the row comes back blank, which is what it meant, and the export prints how many did. A row that stops being marked without a word is the silence D-272 was about. **Six of the ten new tests fail against the pre-fix code** - watched failing before being trusted - and the three pinning the D-272 guard pass against both, which is the evidence the guard was not weakened to get here. **An older workbook still has N cells in it** and they go on meaning not-yet rather than becoming an error, which is its own test. |
| D-283 | 24 Sep 2026 | Statutory data / Calculators (P2, P7) | **`--golden-tests-passed` records that ONE named command passed: `pytest -m golden`.** The `golden` marker now means a figure a regulator PUBLISHED - a SARS or DEL worked example, computed figure or rate - and `statute` means a section's arithmetic transcribed where nothing is published. `test_gross_golden.py`, `test_leave_pay_golden.py` and `test_termination_golden.py` were renamed `_statute` and re-marked. `statutory/tests/test_golden_figures.py` (itself golden) holds every golden literal to `reference/ref-2026.03.01.json`; `statutory/tests/test_golden_command.py` pins the module list and both `--help` texts.
