## Still open

Closed this pass: O-02 (D-119), O-05 (D-120), O-10 (D-121), O-11 (D-122), O-03 (D-125), O-08 (D-126), O-17 (D-135).

| Ref | Area | Decision needed | Needed by | Recommendation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| O-04 | Commercial | Payment gateway | Before P1 | Unanswered. A South African provider supporting debit order as well as cards. Confirm the merchant account and mandate process early - it is usually the slowest part, and D-120's card-upfront trial means the gateway is on the critical path for the first paying customer rather than for launch generally. |
| O-06 | Legal | Who does the labour law review, and when | OVERDUE - was 'engage during P2' | Unanswered, and now the oldest open item on the list. Every statutory figure in this product rests on research that no qualified person has read. It is the one risk that tests cannot catch, because a test only proves the code does what it was told. D-124 has just produced a second example of why: a plausible citation that did not survive being checked. The reviewer should be given the verification workbook and the DECISIONS log, not a demo. O-13 and O-15 are two specific questions waiting for the same person. ADDED 14 Sep 2026: D-148 (the Sunday, public-holiday and standby hour-bucketing shapes) and D-149 (the days_worked_equivalent rule) are two more interpretations with no published worked example behind them, from calculators/attendance.py - not statutory citations that might be wrong like D-124's, but modelling choices that could reasonably have gone another way. ADDED 14 Sep 2026 (D-68 closure): SD1 clause 23(1)(d) [CORRECTED 19 Sep 2026, D-224 - see below] gives pay-in-lieu-of-notice formulas for "one working day's notice" and for "TWO WEEKS' notice" - and no band in the determination, before or after this closure, is two weeks. There is no stated formula anywhere in clause 23 for the four-week band, which is the band that actually applies to almost every contract cleaning termination past the first month. BCEA s38 is the likely fallback - it gives a general pay-in-lieu formula for notice under the Act - but nobody has confirmed SD1 intends that read, and clause 23(1)(d)'s own two-week figure suggests the drafters had something more specific in mind that this determination's consolidated text does not spell out. P7's termination engine MUST NOT encode a reading of this until the review answers it; termination_notice_band stores the notice period itself and nothing about how to pay it in lieu, which is deliberately as far as this closure goes. ADDED 15 Sep 2026 (D-158 corrected): BCEA s37(1) genuinely overlaps itself at exactly ONE YEAR of service — (b) "two weeks, if the employee has been employed for more than six months but not more than one year" and (c)(i) "four weeks, if the employee has been employed for one year or more" both name exactly one year, one calling it "not more than" and the other "or more". This is not a modelling ambiguity like the others on this line; it is the Act's own text naming one instant twice with two different answers. Four weeks (the richer entitlement, i.e. reading (c)(i) as the one that governs the shared instant) is what termination_notice_band loads, chosen and flagged rather than treated as obviously correct — the reviewer should confirm this is the accepted construction (perhaps by reference to how DEL or the CCMA have read the equivalent moment) before P7 relies on it for anything more consequential than notice, such as severance or a dismissal date calculation that hinges on the same one-year mark. ADDED 15 Sep 2026 (P6 chunk 3): `public_holiday_observance.is_observed=FALSE` (D-179) RECORDS an agreement to work a public holiday under BCEA s18(3); it does not MAKE one, and nothing checks that a genuine agreement stands behind a row. `leave/warnings.py`'s `DUE_WINDOW_DAYS=30` (D-178) is a reporting judgement call, not a citation - BCEA s20 gives the six-month grace period itself and nothing about escalation urgency inside it. Neither needs the reviewer's answer before shipping - both are structural, not statutory guesses - but both are exactly the kind of modelling choice this line exists to collect for the eventual pass. QUEUED 17 Sep 2026 (D-193): where BCEA s23(1) lets an employer withhold sick pay for want of a medical certificate, does the absence still consume the employee's s22 sick leave entitlement? The code today deducts it AND leaves it unpaid. ANSWERED by Kobus 17 Sep 2026: unpaid and NOT charged (D-196). QUEUED 17 Sep 2026 (D-194): confirm SD7 clause 21(1) — family responsibility eligibility, longer than four months AND at least four days a week — has not been amended since GN R.1068 of 15 August 2002. The code refuses a domestic worker on three days a week on that reading; no current consolidation could be read. QUEUED 17 Sep 2026 (D-195): may an employer designate an unauthorised absence as paid annual leave without the employee's agreement under BCEA s20? The code offers it as an employer election (`UNAUTHORISED_ABSENCE_TREATMENT=annual_leave`); the default is unpaid and uncharged. ANSWERED by Kobus 17 Sep 2026: yes — annual leave or unpaid leave are the two treatments, the employer's election (D-195). **STILL OPEN (P6 chunk 4d), and it is not the quantum.** Five days for domestic workers is loaded, cited and not in dispute; SD7 cl 21(2) states it. The open question is whether SD7 carries BCEA s27(1)'s ELIGIBILITY conditions — employed longer than four months AND working at least four days a week — or departs from them as it departs on the quantum. It affects the DOMESTIC sector only (SD1 cl 22(1) is verified against the 1 March 2026 consolidation), and D-189 is marked PROVISIONAL for that sector until it is answered. A verbal confirmation does not close it: it closes on clause text from the gazette or two independent mirrors agreeing character for character. **Reviewer deadline: 22 September 2026** — Kobus's "5 days" of 17 Sep 2026, read as a deadline and recorded per the chunk 4d brief; if it meant the five-day quantum instead, the deadline is wrong and the question is unaffected either way, because a deadline does not change a statutory question's status. | **SD1 NOTICE PAY RESOLVED 19 Sep 2026 (D-224): the clause was misread. It gives figures for one working day and for FOUR WEEKS' notice (the figure being double the weekly wage), not "for two weeks" - there is no missing band. SD1's amounts are a floor ("not less than") and BCEA s38 pays more in every case, so the two do not conflict and P7 chunk 5 prices s38 for every band.** **FOUR BCCCI READINGS ADDED 19 Sep 2026 (P2, chunk B). Three are confirmations, because BCEA s49 settles the engineering either way; ONE IS A BLOCKER.** (1) CONFIRMATION - cl 13.3 caps the return from maternity leave at twelve weeks after the birth where the read-in s25(1) gives roughly seventeen and a half, so s49(1)(d) voids it to that extent and it is not loaded (D-244). (2) CONFIRMATION - cl 10.3(a)(i) demands a sick certificate after more than ONE consecutive day where s23(1) says two, so s49(1)(e) voids it and the threshold stays at 2 for Area B; the agreement contradicts itself here too, cl 10.2(a) tracking s23(1) (D-244). (3) CONFIRMATION - cl 13.5 extends clause 13 to a still birth and to a legal adoption under one year old; read as reaching BOTH additive benefits for a still birth and only cl 13.4(a) for an adoption, since an adoptive parent has no confinement for cl 13.2's three prenatal months to precede (D-244). (4) CONFIRMATION - clause 7, 'Prohibition on Further Negotiation', read as prohibiting industrial action in pursuit of improvements and NOT as forbidding an employer to pay above the rate, so the Area B minimum is a floor; LOW priority, raised only because the agreement elsewhere calls its terms 'actual and not minimum standards' (D-246). **(5) BLOCKER - cl 21.1(b) gives two irreconcilable notice periods between four weeks and six months and nothing in the instrument resolves them. `resolve.notice_band()` REFUSES for that window (D-241), so a KwaZulu-Natal employee of that service length cannot be given or paid notice by this system at all until someone with authority answers it. Notice is symmetric, so there is no safe direction to guess in.** **A CONFIRMATION ADDED 20 Sep 2026, not a blocker (D-181).** Does BCEA s22(4)'s "may" permit an employer to DECLINE to reduce the first cycle's entitlement by the sick leave already taken under s22(3) — and is an employer who declines simply granting more than the statutory minimum, rather than doing something the Act does not contemplate? Kobus has read s22(1)-(4) against the Act and confirms the reading this build implements: the cycle runs from commencement, s22(3) is an interim availability calculation and not a second entitlement, and the first cycle resolves to E − A. On that reading the "may" reads awkwardly and would sit better as "must". **The engineering is settled either way** — the code offers the election, defaults to the reduction, and cannot go below the Act — so nothing waits on this. What it decides is HOW MANY PAID SICK DAYS A DOMESTIC WORKER HAS IN HER FIRST THREE YEARS, which is worth having a lawyer's name against rather than a developer's. **ADDED 21 Sep 2026 (D-275): SD1 clause 23(1)(d)(ii) prices TWO weeks' notice ("double the weekly wage") where clause 23(1)(b) gives FOUR weeks' notice from four weeks' service.** Is 23(1)(d) a floor that s38 simply clears, or does the determination contradict itself? Nothing loaded turns on it — payment in lieu is computed from the notice band under s38 — but D-224 asserted there was no such gap on a misreading of the clause, and the gap is real. **ADDED 22 Sep 2026 (D-277), three on the BCCCI notice clause.** (1) Is the one-week limb of clause 21.1(b) an exception for employees on probation, with the two-week limb as the general rule? That is the reading now loaded, and it turns a refusal into two answers. (2) BCEA s38(1) prices pay in lieu of "notice in terms of section 37", and this notice is in terms of an extended collective agreement — does s38 reach it? (3) Clause 21.2(a)(ii) prices the two weeks on the WAGE, s38 pays s35 REMUNERATION; for an employee with allowances those differ, and which governs is not stated.
| O-07 | Validation | A REAL pilot employer - the generated one is accepted but answers a different question | Identify during P5 | Kobus asked for a generated pilot employer with 22 employees to reconcile exercises against. Accepted and worth building - seeded demo data makes every later phase testable and makes the product demonstrable to a prospect. It is NOT what this item asked, and the two must not be allowed to merge. Generated data validates the code against ITSELF: it will reconcile perfectly, because the same assumptions produced both sides. A real cleaning company with 20 to 50 employees and an existing payroll validates it against REALITY, which is the only thing that finds the assumption everybody in this project shares and nobody has questioned. Keep both; this item stays open for the real one. |
| O-12 | POPIA | Drafting the operator agreement | Before launch | Unanswered. The statutory floor is a written contract under s21 requiring s19 security measures and immediate breach notification. Beyond that: scope and purpose, processing on instruction, confidentiality, named security measures, sub-operator terms, a fixed breach notification window, assistance with data subject rights, audit rights capped sensibly, and return-and-delete on termination subject to the five-year SARS retention carve-out that D-121 depends on. Claude can produce a clause-by-clause specification mapping each obligation to the system capability that honours it, for the lawyer to work from. D-123 simplifies this materially: with no transborder flow there is no s72 chapter to draft. |
| O-13 | POPIA | Does the household exemption apply to domestic employers? - BUILD FOR 'NO' WHATEVER THE ANSWER | Before launch | Kobus answered 'Yes'. That may well be right and it is not a safe thing to build on, for a reason that costs nothing to act on: the answer does not change what gets built, because the risks are wildly asymmetric. If the product assumes the exemption applies and it does not, every domestic tenant is an unregistered responsible party, LabourMax is their operator with no s21 contract in place, and the Information Regulator enforces directly against both. If the product assumes the exemption does NOT apply and it does, the cost is some paperwork nobody needed. So: build for non-exempt, and treat the legal answer as something that can only ever make life easier. On the merits the question is genuinely arguable and should still go to the lawyer - POPIA s6(1)(a) excludes processing 'in the course of a purely personal or household activity', and a household that registers as an employer with SARS, deducts PAYE and UIF, files an EMP201 and issues an IRP5 is performing statutory functions, which is not obviously purely personal. |
| O-14 | POPIA | A sub-operator register - still needed, but the hard half is gone | Before launch | D-123 settled South Africa only hosting and operation, which disposes of POPIA s72 entirely: there is no transborder flow to find a basis for. The register itself is still wanted, for its other job - answering an employer's 'who else touches our data' - and now for a third: it is the only artefact that will catch the SA-only constraint being broken, which will happen at the moment somebody adds a US email sender, SMS gateway or error tracker without thinking. A small table holding each vendor that touches employee data, with purpose, location, data categories and processing region. Currently in the open list rather than the schema. |
| O-15 | Legal | Is a BCEA s41 severance payment or a termination gratuity inside 'earnings' for the COIDA return of earnings? - NARROWED, one sentence for the lawyer | Before P7 | Much better documented than it was, and still open. See D-124. The governing instrument is now identified: Government Notice 1843 of 2023, GG 48673, 30 May 2023, under s82(1)(a), s83(2)(a) and s83(8). Its exclusion list does NOT name payments on termination, and its inclusion list is expressly open-ended ('includes but is not limited to') on top of a head definition of 'the remuneration that he/she receives from his/her employer or that accrues to him/her'. So the narrow question is: does a severance payment fall inside that head definition, given it compensates the LOSS of employment rather than work done during the assessment period? False is loaded on the reading that it does not. Ask the lawyer that one sentence, or write to the Compensation Fund, which is faster and free. Do NOT let a P7 golden test encode the current reading as settled. |
| O-16 | Statutory data | What does a clear Priority sheet entitle us to do about verifystatutory, given the bulk sheets are unanswered? | Before P7 | Unanswered. verifystatutory refuses self-verification and records a whole version as checked figure by figure by a second person. The priority subset has been checked by Kobus; the other 216 rows have not been checked by anybody, though those were cross-checked against two independent sources at load time and are on that evidence better supported than the priority rows ever were. Either finish the bulk sheets and verify each of the eight versions properly, which is real hours; or verify on the strength of the priority pass with --allow-self-verification and let the description record exactly that, which is faster and puts a known half-truth in the audit trail. Recommendation: the second is acceptable for development and the pilot, and must be replaced by the first before any employer outside the pilot runs payroll - because in_force_on() cannot see an unverified version, the shortcut is the thing that lets payroll run at all. |
| O-17 | Employee data | CLOSED, resolved by D-135. Should ``employee_recurring_component`` lose ``balance_outstanding`` and ``total_deduction_cap_pct``? A WORKBOOK DEVIATION, so it is raised rather than done | Before P7 | Both columns are in sheet 02 and both are built. A parallel session building the same table on 14 Sep reached the opposite conclusion and removed them, and its reasoning is good enough to record rather than discard. BALANCE_OUTSTANDING: invariant 3 says balances are derived and never stored as editable numbers, and a loan balance is exactly that - it should fall out of the payslip lines that paid it down, not sit in a column somebody can type over. The counter-argument for keeping it is that it is a CACHE, rebuildable from those lines like every other materialised figure in this schema, and P7 has no ledger to derive it from yet - so removing it now leaves loans with nowhere to live until the payroll engine exists. TOTAL_DEDUCTION_CAP_PCT: the BCEA s34 and SD7 ceilings are statutory figures and 'never hard-code a rate' says they belong in reference data with a citation, not as a number an employer types per employee. The counter-argument is weaker: the column really is a per-employee override of a statutory maximum, and no rate is hard-coded by allowing one. EITHER WAY THE WORKBOOK WINS UNTIL KOBUS SAYS OTHERWISE - CLAUDE.md is explicit that a disagreement with sheet 02 is raised and not resolved unilaterally, which is why this was an open item and not a decision. RESOLVED: the workbook wins, both columns stay - see D-135, which also settles the separate schema judgement (O-18) that this item never actually covered. |
| O-18 | Process | Two Claude sessions were writing the same files on 14 Sep. Only one should | Now | The parallel session built ``leave_type`` and the same three P4 tables independently, reaching D-131 against this line's D-128, with different migration filenames and different schema judgements (O-17). Nothing was lost this time only because the files were staged and compared before writing - the previous forced overwrite on 13 Sep destroyed a decision record and was caught hours later by a CI failure that had nothing to do with it. Two sessions on one working tree have no merge step and no lock: the loser is whichever wrote first. Close one, or give each its own branch. |
| O-19 | Attendance | An employee who works an ordinary shift AND goes onto standby afterwards on the SAME calendar day has no attendance_day row that can represent both | Before this becomes a real capture pattern | attendance_day carries is_standby and day_type as independent columns, but one row's worth of ordinary/Sunday/public-holiday bucketing and one row's worth of standby bucketing (D-148) cannot both be expressed at once without inventing a rule sheet 03 does not state for how the two combine on one row. Contract cleaning's SD7 standby shifts are typically overnight after a day off rather than after a worked day, which is presumably why sheet 02 gives this table one row per date rather than one row per shift - but "presumably" is doing real work in that sentence. If an ordinary-day-then-standby-night pattern turns out to be common in practice, the fix is either a second row keyed some other way, or a schema change letting one row carry two shifts, and it should be decided before P5 chunk 2 or 3 builds against an assumption this item never actually confirmed. |
| O-20 | Attendance | working_time_rule_set.standby_window_start/end are loaded, read into RuleFigures, and never consulted by calculators/attendance.py — bucket_day() trusts the is_standby flag on its own, unconditionally | Before P7 prices standby pay | Found while closing D-157: the calculator's is_standby branch computes standby_hours_worked from the worked total alone and never checks it against the gazetted standby window, so a day flagged is_standby is treated as standby pay regardless of what time it actually happened. This may be entirely intentional - the employer is the one asserting "this was a standby occasion," and the window may exist only for future validation or for P7's payroll pricing to read directly - but nobody has confirmed that reading, and it predates this session. Worth a decision one way or the other before P7 turns standby_hours_worked and standby_allowance_per_shift into an amount. |
| O-21 | Statutory data / infrastructure | How does a SCHEMA change to a cited statutory table reach a database that already holds VERIFIED reference data? | Before P11, and before any deployment | Found closing D-158: correcting termination_notice_band's inclusivity required editing two already-loaded fixture files in place (dropping and re-adding columns) and, because their checksums then no longer matched what those exact files had loaded, deleting the affected reference_data_version and child rows from the database by hand before reloading. That is a normal, low-stakes Tuesday on a dev database where nothing is verified yet. It is not available at all once verified_at is set on a version that a payroll run has actually depended on - the rows are the audit record of what a real payslip was computed against, and deleting them to make room for a schema fix is deleting evidence, not tidying data. THE COLLISION: reference/*.json fixture files currently serve two roles at once - they are the BUILD INPUT the tools/build_*.py scripts and the loader treat as freely regenerable, and they are also the closest thing this system has to an AUDIT RECORD of what was actually loaded and verified. A structural fix (adding a column, correcting a table's shape) naturally wants to treat them as the first; POPIA and the whole verification apparatus (D-51, D-56, D-57) requires them to behave as the second. Nobody has designed how those two roles separate once real verified data exists - not proposed here, deliberately, since the answer wants more thought than a chunk closing an unrelated decision should give it. Needs an answer before P11 (retention) and before the first real employer's payroll run makes a termination_rule_set or termination_notice_band row verified in earnest. |
| O-22 | Statutory data | The three remaining zero-means-no-figure sentinel columns | **`night_allowance_value` BEFORE P7; the three standby columns with O-19/O-20** | `night_allowance_value` and `standby_allowance_per_shift` / `standby_window_start` / `standby_window_end` still encode "this instrument states no figure" as zero — the shape D-198 removed from the accommodation cap, where a rule set row written without thinking about the column becomes silently wrong and nothing raises. Same fix: a paired boolean, NOT NULL with no database default, and a nullable figure. **`night_allowance_value` must be fixed BEFORE P7 prices night work** (the payroll engine reads it, and zero currently means both "no statutory figure, read the employer's setting" and would mean "pay nothing"), so its deadline is the start of P7. The three standby columns are tangled with the unresolved standby modelling (O-19, O-20) and cannot be settled until that is — deadline: whenever O-19/O-20 are answered, and before P7 prices standby. Raised 17 Sep 2026 (P6 chunk 5, task 0d); not fixed in that chunk deliberately. **PARTLY CLOSED 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 3, D-218): the night allowance is now a checked enum plus a nullable value, with three CHECK constraints, on the deadline this row set it. The three STANDBY columns remain open and `calculators/gross.py` refuses to price a standby day until they are settled (D-219).** |
| O-23 | Tax | Three PAYE readings a tax practitioner should confirm | Before the first live payroll run, with P2's verification | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 2), each implemented on a cited reading and flagged rather than left implicit. **(a) A directive suppresses the rebates and the medical scheme fees tax credit.** Paragraph 9(3) and "employers may under no circumstances deviate from the instructions of the directive" both point this way, and G20's only explicit interaction rule (apply the percentage before allowable deductions) points the same way - but paragraph 9(6) states the s6A credit against employees' tax generally, and the guide never says in terms whether it survives an IRP3(b). **(b) `TaxStatus.FOREIGN` is taxed on the ordinary tables.** Nationality is an IRP5 identification distinction; relief under a double taxation agreement reaches the payslip as a DIRECTIVE, which is a different status. If instead the column was ever meant to carry a non-resident withholding treatment, this is wrong. **(c) `directive_amount` is taken as the amount for ONE PAY PERIOD.** `employee_tax_profile` carries no unit column, and an IRP3(c) directive is written for the monthly employees both our sectors run - but a weekly-paid employee with a directive stating a monthly amount would be under-deducted by a factor of four, with nothing raising. The cheapest fix if (c) is wrong is a unit column on the tax profile, not a change to the calculator. |
| O-24 | Legal / Payroll | Which BCEA provisions fall away above the earnings threshold | Before any employee above the threshold is paid; not urgent for either target sector | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 3). s6(3) requires the Minister to "make a determination that excludes the application of this Chapter or any provision of it to any category of employees earning in excess of an amount stated in that determination" - so the exclusion list lives in the determination, not in the Act, and it has not been read into this build. CLAUDE.md records one part of the answer as settled (a high earner loses s18(3) and keeps the rest of s18) and `calculators/gross.py` applies exactly that. For s10 (overtime), s16 (Sunday) and s17(2) (the night allowance) the position is unknown, so a period carrying any of those hours for an employee flagged above the threshold RAISES. Two things are needed: the determination's own exclusion list, and the threshold amount as loaded reference data with its citation - the flag itself is already a declared boolean on the input (D-110's shape), so no schema change is expected. Domestic work and contract cleaning are both far below the threshold, so nothing real is blocked. |
| O-25 | Payroll / Legal | Does a contractual annual bonus count toward the s35(4) leave pay average? | Before the first December bonus is paid, and before termination payouts (P7's later chunks) | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 4). `BONUS_PRO_RATA.affects_leave_pay_average` is FALSE, set when the catalogue was seeded. Government Notice 691 includes "(c) any cash payments made to an employee, except those listed as exclusions" and excludes "(e) discretionary payments not related to an employee's hours of work or performance" and "(c) gratuities ... and gifts from the employer". **A discretionary ex gratia bonus is plainly excluded. SD1's December bonus is not discretionary** - clause 24 gazettes 4.333 weeks' wages, pro-rated on termination - so on GN 691's own wording it reads as an included cash payment, and the current flag would then understate leave pay for every contract cleaning employee whose thirteen-week window contains December. Against that: a bonus is not remuneration "for working for a period equal to the period of annual leave" in any per-day sense, and including it makes leave taken in January worth materially more than the same leave taken in March. **Not changed here**, because the flag is a settled decision and CLAUDE.md requires raising one before changing it. The fix, if the reading is wrong, is a data change to one seeded row plus its reason - no schema change, and no change to `calculators/leave_pay.py`, which never decides this. |
| O-26 | Payroll / Employees | BCEA s84(1): previous employment is not aggregated anywhere | Before any re-hired employee is terminated, and before P7's assembly reads service length | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 5). s84(1): "For the purposes of determining the length of an employee's employment with an employer FOR ANY PROVISION OF THIS ACT, previous employment with the same employer must be taken into account if the break between the periods of employment is less than one year." `EmployeeEngagement.service_days_to()` is explicitly this-engagement-only and its own docstring says "continuous service across a re-hire is a different question with a different answer"; `employees/engagements.py` returns exactly that figure and nothing aggregates. So a returning employee re-hired after six months gets the wrong NOTICE BAND (understated) and the wrong SEVERANCE (understated - completed years restart at zero), both in the direction that underpays. D-103 is not the problem and must not be undone: a re-hire is correctly a new engagement ROW. What is missing is a service-length function that sums engagements where the gap is under a year. **It interacts with D-163**, which anchors leave cycles to the CURRENT engagement and never revives the prior one - s84(2) says any leave granted in a previous period must be taken into account - so the leave half needs the labour law review (O-06) before it is touched. The notice and severance half does not: it is arithmetic over engagement rows. `calculators/termination.py` takes the years as an input and documents this rather than accepting a figure computed the wrong way in silence. |
| O-27 | Payroll / Schema | BCEA s40(a) time off in lieu has nowhere to be recorded | Before the first termination of an employee who has banked time off | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 5). s40 opens "On termination of employment, an employer MUST pay an employee - (a) for any paid time off that the employee is entitled to in terms of section 10(3) or 16(3) that the employee has not taken". s10(3) is time off agreed instead of overtime pay (30 minutes per overtime hour, or 90 minutes in place of it) and s16(3) is the same for Sunday work. Nothing in this schema records either: the seeded `leave_type` catalogue has no time-off-in-lieu code, and no ledger holds the balance. So the payout cannot include an amount s40(a) makes mandatory. Both sections require an AGREEMENT, so a household employer who has never made one is unaffected, which is why this is not blocking - but an employer who has is short-paying leavers with nothing to show it. The likely shape is a further `leave_type` with its own ledger balance in hours, priced at the ordinary rate, which would then flow through the existing s40 machinery; `calculators/termination.py` does not take an input for it, because an input nothing can populate is a silent zero dressed up as coverage. |
| O-28 | Schema / Process | ~~The payslip tables were built WITHOUT sheet 02 in front of them~~ **CLOSED 24 Sep 2026 by D-289** - reconciled, and held there by `payroll/tests/test_sheet_02_reconciliation.py` | Before P7's assembly writes the first payslip | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 6). CLAUDE.md is explicit that sheet 02 of `Labourmax-HR_Database_Specification.xlsx` "is the source of truth for every model" and that where this repo and the workbook disagree, the workbook wins. The workbook is kept outside the repo and was not available to the session that built `payroll_run`, `payslip`, `payslip_line` and `ytd_accumulator`, so all four were designed from the invariants, the existing schema's own shapes and the PHASES line - not transcribed. Every earlier table in this build WAS reconciled against sheet 02 (D-82 settled a design question against it, D-170 reconciled `leave_transaction` column by column, D-135 restored two columns a parallel session had removed because the workbook says keep them). These four have not been. **What to do**: open sheet 02 at these four tables and diff the columns. A column the workbook has and these models do not is the likely finding, and adding one later is an ordinary migration; a column these models have that the workbook does not is fine if it is justified (D-144's own precedent) but should be recorded as such. Nothing is deployed and no payslip exists, so the cost of a correction is currently zero - which is exactly why it should happen before the assembly starts writing rows. |
| O-29 | Attendance / Payroll | `attendance_day.locked_by_payroll_run_id_ref` is still a forward reference | Whenever the three apps are next touched together; not urgent | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P7 chunk 7). P5 created the column as a nullable `BigIntegerField` named for what it would become, because `payroll_run` did not exist. It exists now, and `payroll/runs.py::finalise()` populates the column with the real run's pk - so the data is right and only the TYPE is still a placeholder. Converting it to a real foreign key is mechanical but touches nine references across three apps (`attendance/capture.py`, `attendance/importing.py`, `attendance/models.py`, `leave/authorisation.py`, `leave/models.py` and four test modules), which is why it was not folded into this chunk: the churn would have been larger than the chunk it rode along with, for a naming improvement rather than a correctness one. **The risk while it stays**: nothing enforces that the id points at a run that exists. Low, because `payroll_run` rows are never deleted - `payslip` PROTECTs its run and a reversal OneToOnes it - but it is the kind of low risk that stops being low the day something starts deleting draft runs. `payroll_calculation_trace.payslip_id_ref` was the same shape and was converted in chunk 6 (D-208's own promise), which is the precedent for doing this one. |
| O-30 | Statutory data | The BCCCI Main Agreement in force BEFORE 1 April 2026 is not loaded | Before any KwaZulu-Natal contract cleaning payroll for a period ending in March 2026 | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P2, BCCCI chunk A). The new agreement takes effect 1 April 2026, so 1-31 March 2026 has no loaded rate for contract cleaning Area B. **That month is not a legal vacuum, and this is how we know a document exists to go and find**: clause 2(1)(a) of the new agreement provides that 'the parties agree that the current Main Agreement shall continue to be enforced', and clause 2(3) that 'upon expiration of this agreement and in the absence of a new agreement, the terms and conditions prevailing at the time of expiration shall apply until such time as a new agreement has been promulgated'. A predecessor BCCCI Main Collective Agreement therefore governed March 2026. It is not held by this project. `resolve.minimum_wage()` REFUSES for that period rather than answering the National Minimum Wage (D-238), so nothing is silently underpaid - the cost of the gap is that a KZN employer cannot run a March 2026 payroll at all, which is the correct cost. **To close it**: obtain the preceding gazette from the BCCCI or the Department, load it as its own version with its own applies_from, and the refusal stops firing on its own. | **FOUND 20 Sep 2026 (P2 close-out), and NOT YET LOADED.** It is the Main Collective Agreement extended by NOTICE 1726 OF 2023 in Government Gazette 48356, 31 March 2023, signed by Minister T W Nxesi — same council, same parties, same structure as the 2026 agreement. Its clause 2(1) gives the same commencement rule ("the 1st day of the month following the date of promulgation", so 1 April 2023) and the extension notice carries the same open end ("shall remain in force until replaced by a subsequent agreement"), which is what makes it the instrument governing March 2026. **Clause 4.1(a), read off the gazette: R27,50 an hour from the period of operation; R29,12 from 1 March 2024; R30,86 from 1 March 2025.** So the figure the refusal is missing is R30,86, and it is still in force on 31 March 2026 because no successor had taken effect. Two corroborations: R30,86 is above the National Minimum Wage of R30,23 in force on 1 March 2026, as a bargaining council rate must be, and R30,86 to the new agreement's R32,40 is a 4,99% increase, which is the shape of the other steps. Clause 4.3's 10% night allowance and clause 4.5's 4,33 bonus are unchanged from this agreement to the next. **Deliberately not loaded in the same pass as being found**: a wage rate gets a transcription and a verification against the source, not a load off the back of one reading, and the rest of the agreement has not been read clause by clause yet. The PDF is at gov.za/sites/default/files/gcis_document/202304/48356gen1726.pdf. **CLOSED 20 Sep 2026 by D-259.** The predecessor was found (Notice 1726 of 2023 in GG 48356, 31 March 2023), transcribed, reported, and then loaded in a separate pass. March 2026 resolves R30,86 with the 4,33 monthly factor, and the two agreements abut at 1 April 2026 with neither a gap nor an overlap. **What remains is narrower than this item was**: only the wage rates and the factor were read from that gazette, so Area B's March 2026 leave, working time, termination and notice rules still fall back to Sectoral Determination 1 rather than to the agreement that governed them. That is unchanged from before and is now the only part of March 2026 that is an approximation.
| O-31 | Statutory data / Payroll scope | Three KwaZulu-Natal-only payroll lines and four KwaZulu-Natal-only leave types are recorded and NOT built | Before a KwaZulu-Natal contract cleaning payroll is run in anger | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P2, BCCCI chunk B). **Payroll lines**: the provident fund (cl 31.3, 6% of monthly wage from the member, matched by the employer, excepting casual and temporary cleaners and those inside the four-month probation); the council levy (cl 19.1, 0,5% of monthly basic wage from each non-casual employee, matched by the employer, remitted by the 7th); and the Family Medical Crisis Plan (cl 35, R54,00 a month from the employee and R54,00 from the company, with cl 35.2(c) providing for the premium to change on a month's notice, so it is effective-dated data rather than a constant). **All three interact with the BCEA s34 deduction total and with D-198's accommodation ceiling**, and none of that interaction is built. **Leave types**: study leave (cl 12, one day to prepare and one to write each examination, on full pay, on satisfactory proof); the prenatal clinic day (cl 13.2 — the FIGURES are loaded under D-244, the leave type that spends them is not); shop steward leave (cl 20.4, four paid days a year for an office bearer of a representative trade union, six for any other shop steward); and the 28-day long-service band (cl 9.1(b) — the rule set columns and the resolver are built under D-243, the accrual engine does not read them yet). Also unbuilt: cl 8.6's compressed week, cl 8.7's four-month averaging, cl 16.1's transport duty after 20:00, and cl 16.2's night restriction on an employee more than six months pregnant. | **ALL THREE FIGURES ARE UNCHANGED IN THE PREDECESSOR, 20 Sep 2026 (D-265)**, read off Notice 1726 of 2023 in GG 48356: the provident fund at 6% with the same two exceptions (cl 30.2-30.3), the council levy at 0,5% of monthly basic wage for each non-casual employee matched by the employer and remitted by the 7th (cl 18.1, word for word with 2026's cl 19.1), and the Family Medical Crisis Plan at R54,00 a month each side (cl 34.1(b), 34.2(a)). The amounts above were read only off the 2026 gazette when this was raised, so this extends them backwards to 1 April 2023 rather than leaving March 2026 to be guessed at. **2026 adds one obligation the predecessor does not have** (cl 31.4): a company exempted from the provident fund must send proof of payment and monthly schedules to the Council Secretary by the 15th and its Compliance Officer's details within 30 days, or the exemption is withdrawn. **THE 28-DAY BAND IS CLOSED, 20 Sep 2026 (D-267)**: `leave/cycles.py` reads `resolve.annual_leave_days()` and passes the employee's wage area, so a long-serving Area B employee's cycle now carries the band instead of Sectoral Determination 1's figure. The remaining items in this entry are unchanged. **THE THREE LEAVE TYPES ARE LOADED, 20 Sep 2026 (D-268)**: study leave, shop steward leave and the prenatal clinic day now have catalogue rows, area-scoped figures for both agreements, and resolvers. **What remains of the leave half is ENFORCEMENT, not data**: nothing caps the three prenatal days or the 4/6 shop steward days, because neither type keeps a balance and the application layer does not read the resolvers; and no column records whether an employee is an office bearer of a representative trade union, so the caller must state it. The three PAYROLL LINES above are unchanged and still unbuilt. **THE TWO CAPS ARE ENFORCED, 20 Sep 2026 (D-269)**, and the office-bearer status now has a home (`employee_union_role`). What remains of this item is the three PAYROLL LINES — the provident fund, the council levy and the Family Medical Crisis Plan premium — none of which is built, and none of which interacts yet with the BCEA s34 deduction total or D-198's accommodation ceiling.
| O-32 | Statutory data / Legal reading | The BCCCI gazette's own drafting errors, recorded so nobody "corrects" a citation into agreement with them | Whenever a BCCCI citation is next checked against the gazette | Raised 19 Sep 2026 (P2, BCCCI chunk B). Every one verified page by page against GN R.7296 in GG 54412. **Wrong cross-references**: cl 3's definition of "overtime" points at clause 7 for maximum normal hours, but clause 7 is Prohibition on Further Negotiation and ordinary hours are cl 8.3; cl 4.6.1.2 cites "section (29)" for retirement, which is clause 30 — clause 29 is desertion. **A wrong word**: cl 36.1 defines operational requirements as the needs "of an EMPLOYEE" where BCEA s41(1) says employer. **Four duplicate sub-letter runs**: cl 4.5 has two c), cl 21.1(b) has two i), clause 31 has two 31.3, and cl 35.2 runs a) then c). The sub-lettering of 4.5 is off by one from (d) as a result, which is why the December bonus clauses are cited in this build by their corrected letters with the published ones named alongside. **None of these is a figure**, so none changes a number — they change which clause a citation should read, and a later reader with the gazette in hand would otherwise "fix" a correct citation into agreement with a typo. | **FOUR MORE FOUND IN CHUNK C's verification pass, all against the gazette.** cl 10.2(a) drops a word: "An employer IS required to pay ... if the employee has been absent from work for one day ... and ... does not produce a medical certificate", where BCEA s23(1) says an employer is NOT required to pay - read literally the clause compels payment in the very case it exists to excuse. cl 13.1(b)(ii) has the medical certificate certify the employee "fit to work until four weeks prior to the expected date of confinement" where cl 13.3 compels her to cease working "at least 8 weeks prior" - four against eight, in adjacent sub-clauses. Clause 4.6's own sub-letters run e), f), c), i), ii), d), e), f), g), h), so (e) and (f) each appear TWICE and the run restarts at c) after f). And cl 4.6(h) names "Clause 4.5 c) ii; 4.5 e); and 4.5 g)" while the three rules it is plainly about are 4.6's OWN c) ii), e) and g) - under 4.5's real letters that list would make the discretion clause subject to itself. **The 4.5 lettering the previous brief corrected is right and is what this build cites**: 4.5 genuinely carries two c), so its discretion clause is 4.5(g) and it names 4.5(c)(ii), 4.5(d) and 4.5(f) - verified word for word. **THE CLAUSE 7 CROSS-REFERENCE IS EXPLAINED, 20 Sep 2026 (D-260).** It is a renumbering leftover, not a typo: the PREDECESSOR agreement (Notice 1726 of 2023 in GG 48356) numbers its working time clause 7, so the definition was correct when it was written. The 2026 agreement inserted "5. MINIMUM HOURS" and pushed every later clause down by one without updating the definition. That is worth knowing because it says where else to look — every other internal cross-reference in the 2026 agreement to a clause numbered 5 or above is suspect for the same reason. **BOTH WRONG CROSS-REFERENCES ARE RENUMBERING LEFTOVERS AND BOTH ARE NOW EXPLAINED (D-260, D-261).** In the predecessor (Notice 1726 of 2023 in GG 48356) clause 7 IS Regulation of Working Time and clause 29 IS Retirement, so clause 3's "overtime" definition pointing at clause 7, and clause 4.6.1.2 citing "section (29)" for retirement, were both correct when written. The 2026 agreement inserted "5. MINIMUM HOURS" and pushed every later clause down by one without updating either reference. **Two more errors turn out to be inherited rather than new**: clause 35.1 of the 2023 agreement already defines operational requirements as the needs "of an EMPLOYEE" where BCEA s41(1) says employer, and clause 20.1(b) already prints two items as "i)". **One is genuinely new in 2026**: clause 4.5's duplicate c) and the clause 4.6 restatement do not exist in the predecessor, whose clause 4.5 is cleanly lettered a) to g) — which independently confirms the corrected lettering this build cites.
| O-33 | Statutory data / Citations | Two instruments are cited under two different spellings each, so the verification workbook shows them as four source documents instead of two | Next time either fixture is re-encoded | Raised 20 Sep 2026 (P2 close-out), found by grouping the workbook by source document. The BCCCI Main Agreement is cited as "Bargaining Council for the Contract Cleaning Services Industry (KwaZulu-Natal) Main Collective Agreement, GN R.7296 in Government Gazette 54412, 27 March 2026" on 67 figures and as "BCCCI (KwaZulu-Natal) Main Collective Agreement, GN R.7296 in GG 54412, 27 March 2026" on 23 — the short form was used in the notice band fixture to fit `source_reference`'s 200-character limit. The SARS code guide is cited as "SARS Guide for Codes Applicable to Employees Tax Certificates (PAYE-AE-06-G06), 2026 issue" on 114 figures and as "SARS PAYE-AE-06-G06, Guide for Codes Applicable to Employees Tax Certificates 2026, revision 13, effective 19 September 2025" on 12. **Neither is wrong and no figure is affected** — both name the same document findably, which is what the citation rule requires. The cost is that a verifier opens each of those two documents twice. Harmonising them is a notes-and-citations change, which is exactly what `loadstatutory --supersede` permits, so it can ride along with the next re-encode of those fixtures rather than justifying one of its own. Do not "fix" it by loosening the workbook's grouping into fuzzy matching: two citations that differ should look different until somebody decides they are the same. | **CLOSED 20 Sep 2026 by D-257.** Both pairs were the same instrument, read before merging: the BCCCI pair share GN R.7296 and GG 54412, the SARS pair share the guide code PAYE-AE-06-G06. Normalised in the fixtures and superseded with no figure changed, 19 source documents becoming 17, and `checkstatutory` now reports the next pair rather than waiting for somebody to notice it in a workbook.
| O-34 | Statutory data / Schema | `leave_rule_set`'s three `parental_leave_*` columns are NOT NULL, read by nothing, and duplicate a dedicated table | Before anything is written that reads them | Raised 20 Sep 2026 (P2), found while loading a rule set that spans the Van Wyk judgment. `parental_leave_total_months`, `parental_leave_additional_days` and `parental_leave_shareable` are declared on the model and referenced by no other line of application code: `parental_leave_quantum` (D-201) is the authoritative table and is separately effective-dated, currently from 3 October 2025 to 3 October 2028. **The duplication is not harmless.** The predecessor BCCCI rule set runs from 1 April 2023 and therefore spans the day the Van Wyk interim reading-in took effect, and these columns cannot express what applied before it — there was no four-months-plus-ten-days aggregate and s25A's standalone ten days was a different entitlement entirely. The row carries the same values every other rule set carries because the columns are NOT NULL and it has to be created; its note says in terms that they are not a statement about the pre-Van Wyk position. **The moment somebody reads them they will be reading a figure nobody verified for that period.** Either drop the three columns and let the quantum table be the only answer, or make them nullable and leave them empty where the rule set's period does not match a single quantum row. Do not simply populate them more carefully — the problem is that there are two places to look. |
| O-35 | Tooling / Statutory data | `tools/build_notice_band_fixture.py` no longer produces the fixture on disk, so regenerating it would change the checksum for no substantive reason | Next time that fixture is re-encoded | Raised 20 Sep 2026 (P2 close-out), found by comparing every converted builder's output against its committed file after D-264. Eight of nine match byte for byte. `ref-2026.03.01-notice-bands.json` does not: the builder now emits `sector_area`, `is_contested` and `contested_reason` on each of its seven rows — the columns D-240 and D-241 added — and the committed file predates them. **The loaded DATA is identical**, because all three are at their defaults and the loader fills them either way, which is why nothing has ever failed. The cost is narrow and specific: `statutory/tests/test_fixture_encoding.py` tells a reader to rebuild a damaged fixture by running its builder, and for this one file that would change the checksum and make `check_fixture_checksums()` refuse the loaded version by name, needing an `-r2` supersede for a change that alters no figure. Close it by regenerating and superseding the next time that fixture is touched for a real reason — not on its own, which would spend a version label on nothing. |
| O-36 | Leave / Schema | `leave_type.is_statutory` cannot say "statutory for some employees", which is now the ordinary case | Before anything reads the column | Raised 20 Sep 2026 (P6, D-268). The flag is a catalogue-wide boolean on a SHARED row, and one sector is now governed by two instruments (D-240), so the same leave type can be compulsory for one employer and a discretionary benefit for the next. **STUDY is seeded `is_statutory=False`** — right for every employer who grants study leave as a benefit, and wrong for a KwaZulu-Natal contract cleaner, whose agreement compels it at clause 12 on full pay. It cannot be corrected in place: a system row is locked against UPDATE (D-93) and seeding never updates. PRENATAL and SHOP_STEWARD were therefore seeded TRUE rather than given a known-wrong value for symmetry with a row that cannot be fixed, so the catalogue is now inconsistent on purpose and a test pins both values with the reason. **Nothing computes a wrong answer from it today**: the column is read by no application code at all — only set in fixtures and tests — which is why this is an open item and not a defect. Close it by dropping the column and letting the rule set answer whether an instrument grants a leave type, which is where every other such question already lives; or by scoping it, which means a second table. Do not "fix" STUDY by re-seeding, because an environment that has already seeded would then differ from a fresh one. |
| O-37 | Statutory data / Checks | `checkstatutory` does not report a development verifier that is a SECONDARY checker | Before the human pass records a shared version | Raised 20 Sep 2026 (P2), found while splitting the superseded ticks out of the machine-verifier warning (D-271) and deliberately NOT fixed in that chunk, whose brief said the live check stays exactly as it is. D-270 let a version carry several checkers in `verified_by_users`, and widened both halves of the GATE to match: `is_machine_verified` and `unusable_q()` treat a version as machine-verified if ANY checker is a development identity. `check_machine_verified_versions()` was not widened with them — it still filters on `verified_by_user`, the verifier of record — so a version whose machine identity is the SECOND name on it is refused by the payroll gate and never mentioned by `checkstatutory`. **Nothing unsafe follows**: the gate is the guard and it holds, so no payslip can be computed against such a version. What is lost is the standing reminder — somebody would see a run refuse and have no report telling them which version to re-verify. Close it by having the check read the same set the gate reads, which is one filter, plus a test that a machine as the second checker is reported. **CLOSED 20 Sep 2026.** `ReferenceDataVersion.machine_verified_q()` is now one expression on the model, composed into `unusable_q()` and used directly by `check_machine_verified_versions()`, so the gate and the report ask the same question of the same rows. The message names the MACHINE identities rather than `verified_by_user`, who with several checkers is often a real person who did nothing wrong. A property test pins it shut: whatever the gate treats as machine-verified, the report names — live ones as warnings, superseded ones in D-271's note. **Found on closing it**: migration 0024 had never been applied to the development database, so the whole M2M path — the gate's EXISTS included — had only ever run against the test database, which rebuilds from migrations every time. `checkstatutory` failed with "relation does not exist" the moment the report started reading it. Run `migrate` after adding a migration, not only `pytest`. |
| O-38 | Statutory data / Citations | Ten cited documents have NO source URL, one URL does not resolve, and two source PDFs have no readable clause numbers | Before or during the human pass — each one costs the verifier time | Raised 21 Sep 2026 (P2), found by running `manage.py fetchsources` over the whole corpus (D-273). **A citation whose reader cannot reach the document is doing half its job**, and these are why only 5 of 156 check groups open at a page and 78 open at nothing. **(a) TEN documents carry no `source_url` at all**, so nothing can be fetched and no retry helps: the Compensation Fund earnings notice, the UIF s6(2) determination (GG 44641), GN 5970 (GG 52232), GN 7384 (GG 54544), the Public Holidays Act 36 of 1994, Sectoral Determination 1, Sectoral Determination 7, the Skills Development Levies Act 9 of 1999, the Unemployment Insurance Contributions Act 4 of 2002 and the Value-Added Tax Act 89 of 1991. Several of these carry many figures — SD7 and the Public Holidays Act between them account for a large share of the unlinked groups. **(b) ONE url does not resolve**: Form BCEA1A, the Summary of the Act, cited to `wits.ac.za`, fails TLS verification (certificate chain). Citing a university's copy of a government form is fragile in itself; the Department publishes it. **(c) TWO PDFs have no usable text layer** — both BCCCI Main Agreements. They hold ~99 000 characters of extractable text in which the clause numbers do not survive the font encoding, so no page can be found by searching and those ~40 groups link to the document only. Nothing to fix in the citation; it would need OCR, which is a much bigger thing than this. **Adding a URL is a PROSE change**, so it rides on `loadstatutory --supersede` without a new figure (D-199), and `fetchsources` re-run afterwards reports the improvement. **EIGHT OF THE TEN ARE CLOSED, 21 Sep 2026 (D-274)**, each URL fetched and the document confirmed to be the one cited; the Wits copy of Form BCEA1A resolved on a later attempt, so its TLS failure was intermittent rather than a dead link. **What remains is (a) SD1 and SD7, whose citations name a consolidated text that is not published anywhere — only the amending notices are, and linking one of those would be the wrong document — and (c) the two BCCCI gazettes' unreadable text layer.** Those 44 groups open at nothing and ~40 more at the document only. **SD7 IS CLOSED, 21 Sep 2026 (D-275)** — the citation now names GN R.1068 in RG 7434 (GG 23732), 15 August 2002 and carries its URL, so 19 of the 20 cited documents download. **SD1 alone remains**, and not for want of a candidate: there are THREE conflicting identities for its notice and naming one would be a coin toss dressed as a citation. **A FOURTH AND BETTER CANDIDATE, 23 Sep 2026 (D-279):** D-194 records reading a consolidation headed "(1 March 2026 - to date)" whose own source line is "Government Notice 622 in Government Gazette 20064 dated 14 May 1999" — a gazette NUMBER, which none of the three earlier candidates carried, and a date two of them agree on. Not acted on in that pass; it is where the next attempt should start. |
| O-39 | Statutory data / Leave | The three `parental_leave_*` columns on `leave_rule_set` are read by nothing, cited to instruments that do not state them, and carry a court-ordered interim figure with no end date | Before the human pass reaches the rule set rows — they are three figures per rule set that nobody can verify against the cited source | Raised 23 Sep 2026 (D-279). `parental_leave_total_months` (4), `parental_leave_additional_days` (10) and `parental_leave_shareable` (true) sit on every `leave_rule_set` row. **Nothing reads them** — `tools/build_bccci_2023_fixture.py` says so in terms, and the live source is `parental_leave_quantum` (D-201, D-202). **They are a stale duplicate of a suspended reading-in**: the Van Wyk interim order runs out on 3 October 2028 and `parental_leave_quantum` is effective-dated to end there so the quantum REFUSES rather than falling back (D-203); these columns carry no end date, so on 4 October 2028 they will still read 4 months and 10 days when the authoritative table has stopped answering. **And they are unverifiable as cited**: three figures per rule set that the cited instrument does not state — SD1 has no parental clause, and neither does SD7. **Three options.** (a) DROP the columns. Correct, and it costs a migration plus every rule set fixture re-encoded and superseded — six versions, all prose-and-schema — plus `leave_rules()` and the test fixtures that construct a rule set. Nothing reads them, so no behaviour changes; the risk is the churn itself, six supersedes over rows Kobus is part-way through verifying. (b) KEEP them and stop exporting them as checks, by naming them in a not-a-figure list `verification.lines()` skips. Cheap and reversible, and it is a lie by omission: the columns stay in the schema, still duplicating a figure that will expire, and the next person to read the model finds three plausible numbers nothing verified. (c) Do (b) now and (a) after the human pass. **RECOMMENDED: (c).** The verification pass is the thing in flight and six supersedes across it is the one cost worth avoiding right now; dropping the columns is a ten-minute change whenever the corpus is quiet. Until then the citation says `parental: BCEA s25`, which at least sends the reader to the right instrument. **Do not leave it at (b)** — a column that exists and is excluded from verification is exactly the shape D-198 and D-110 keep warning about. |
| O-40 | Attendance / Pay | A day that is BOTH a Sunday and a public holiday has one `day_type`, and nothing decides which section prices it | Before P7 chunk 8 prices a payslip line | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-280). Until now a public holiday could not also be a Sunday in this build, because the Sunday was never loaded. It can now, three times in the loaded corpus, and `attendance_day.day_type` holds exactly one value - so a contract cleaner's 9 August 2026 is captured as SUNDAY and priced under s16, or as PUBLIC_HOLIDAY and priced under s18, and the two give different money. **Three readings, and the Act does not obviously settle between them.** (a) s18 governs and s16 falls away, which is the higher figure in every case this build can construct and is what capturing it as PUBLIC_HOLIDAY already does. (b) The higher of the two, computed and compared - which needs both to be computed, and the calculator's buckets are disjoint by design (D-216). (c) s18(2)(b)(ii) stacks them: "the amount referred to in paragraph (a) plus the amount earned by the employee for the work performed on that day", where "the amount earned" for a Sunday is the s16 amount - a daily wage plus double time. `calculators/gross.py` implements (a) with (ii) read at the ORDINARY rate, which is defensible and is not a reading anybody recorded making. **Nothing is wrong today**: no payslip line exists (P7 chunk 8), and `attendance/capture.py` warns by name on every such day rather than choosing quietly. Close it with the labour law review (O-06). If the answer is (c), `DayPay` needs a public-holiday fact independent of `day_type` and the disjoint-bucket rule needs revisiting - which is the expensive answer, and the reason to ask before building. |
| O-41 | Statutory data / Watch calendar | `PUBLIC_HOLIDAYS` was confirmed five days AFTER the proclamation it missed was gazetted, and a watch row cannot be corrected by a loader | Before the next proclaimed day | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-280). The watch item EXISTS and is right: `as_proclaimed`, and its description says in terms that "election days and days of mourning are proclaimed with little notice and follow no rule". It did not help. Proclamation Notice 346 of 2026 was gazetted 8 September 2026; `last_confirmed_date` reads 13 September 2026, so the sweep ran AFTER publication and recorded a clean check over a calendar that was already a day short. **So the gap is not a missing row.** `next_expected_date` is 2026-12-01 and the description says "load the next calendar year each December" - a December cadence written onto the one item whose whole point is that it has none, leaving nine months in which a proclamation is found only by somebody happening to look. It carries no `source_url` either, so there is no named place to sweep. **And it cannot be corrected in place**: the loader never updates a loaded row, so advancing `last_confirmed_date` or changing the cadence needs a new version, a console screen or a hand edit. **Two candidate closes.** (a) A `checkstatutory` warning when an `as_proclaimed` item's `last_confirmed_date` is older than some window - cheap, and the window is a reporting judgement with no citation behind it, D-178's `DUE_WINDOW_DAYS` shape. (b) Give the item a real source (the Government Gazette proclamations feed) and a monthly sweep, which is a process change rather than a code one. Neither was built here: the brief asked whether a watch item would have caught this, and the honest answer is that one existed, was ticked, and did not. |
| O-42 | Statutory data / Verification | An "N" in the workbook is imported by nothing and then refuses every future export | Next time somebody uses the dropdown | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-281). The Checked cell's data validation offers `Y,N,QUERY` and its error text reads "Y: every figure on this row matches the document. N: not yet. QUERY: one or more is wrong - say which in Note." `IMPORTED_AS` maps Y and QUERY only, so an N is dropped without a word - which is RIGHT, since not-yet is not evidence of anything. The trouble is the other end: `exportverification` counts any non-blank Checked cell as a mark, finds no record of the N in the database, and refuses the export as work it will not throw away, naming "delete the file by hand" as the way out. So the one value that means "I have not done this" is the only one that makes the file un-refreshable. **Three candidate closes.** (a) Have the export's "would be discarded" count ignore N, which is one condition and matches what N means. (b) Record N as its own `ReferenceFigureCheck` outcome - honest, and it makes a deferral evidence, which it is not. (c) Drop N from the dropdown and let blank mean not-yet, which is what blank already means. **RECOMMENDED: (a) plus (c)** - two lines, and it removes the value rather than teaching people around it. Carrying the N across by hand is the workaround in the meantime and is what was done here. **CLOSED 24 Sep 2026 (D-282).** (a) and (c) both, plus a third thing neither option named: the vocabulary moved into `statutory/verification.py` so the dropdown, the `--force` guard and the importer read ONE list. Recommending (a)+(c) had treated the symptom - it was two command modules each owning half the answer, and a dropdown literal owning a third. A case bug came out with it: the importer upper-cased the cell and the export did not. |
| O-43 | Tax | The medical scheme fees tax credit on WEEKLY and FORTNIGHTLY pay: SARS's worked example divides the monthly credit by 4 and by 2; `calculators/paye.py` spreads twelve months' credit over 52 and 26 | Before the first weekly-paid employee with a medical scheme is run | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-283). PAYE-GEN-01-G01 rev 16 §6 p5: "Less: Medical Scheme Fees Tax Credit (R376+R376 p/m ÷ 4 weeks) R 188 … Amount of Tax to be deducted R 88", and "÷ 2 weeks" R376 for the fortnightly example. Over 52 weeks SARS's conversion credits R9 776, one month more than the R9 024 that twelve months of s6A(2) credit comes to; ours credits R173,54 a week and deducts R102,97 against SARS's R88. The annual assessment reconciles either way; what differs is the employee's weekly cash. Not changed: nothing here says which conversion a statutory-rates program must use, and G01 §5 permits "small differences" - but 17% of the week's tax is not small. Ask a tax practitioner, or SARS, one sentence: "for a weekly-paid employee, is the medical credit per week the monthly credit × 12 ÷ 52, or ÷ 4?" The two G01 examples are strict xfails in `test_paye_golden_2027.py`, so `pytest -m golden` prints them every run and the day the code changes they fail and must be removed. Domestic workers are often paid weekly and rarely carry a medical scheme, which is why this is not a blocker today. |
| O-44 | Legal | SD1 clause 3(3): does a long-serving employee who leaves mid-year get the WHOLE bonus, or the months of the current cycle? | Before the first contract cleaning termination is paid | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-286). Clause 3(3): "An annual bonus will be paid to all employees, during the month of December or on termination of employment ... (a) ... four point three three three weeks of the employee's weekly wage ... (b) Subject to paragraph (a) an employee who has not been in employment for a period of 12 months shall be paid a prorated bonus". Read literally, (b) pro-rates only for somebody with under twelve months' SERVICE, so a cleaner with five years who leaves in June would be owed all of (a) - R6 487,15 rather than the R2 702,98 built. **Built: the cycle reading** - the months of the current January-December cycle - because it is the workbook's (sheet 02, `termination_payout.bonus_months_worked`: "Full calendar months in the current bonus cycle"), the loaded note's, and the only reading under which the December bonus and the leaver's bonus are not paid twice for the same months. Nothing in the clause states a cycle at all. Also for the reviewer: whether December itself counts at the December payment (built: yes - the payment is priced as at 31 December, the employee being "in employment" in the month it is paid). One sentence for the lawyer: "Under SD1 3(3), is a mid-year leaver with more than 12 months' service owed the full 4,333 weeks, or a pro rata share of the current year?" |
| O-45 | Statutory data / Verification | A row DELETED from the workbook's Checks sheet is not caught as stale | Before the next verification pass | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-288). The corpus fingerprint (D-272) is written to the Summary sheet at export and compared on import, so it proves the DATABASE has not moved - not that the Checks sheet still carries every group. A workbook arrived with 167 rows against 168 groups (`grp:adoption_age_limit:2:1` missing) and imported cleanly, the gap surfacing only as "PARENTAL-2 - 6 of 7 checked". Nothing was wrongly verified: a missing row reads as outstanding, so the failure is safe in direction. The guard would be one comparison on import - the Checks sheet's group keys against `check_groups()` - refusing with the missing keys named, as a stale file is refused. Not built: nothing asked for it, and the pass that found it is closed. |
| O-46 | Payroll / Schema | Two DECLARED facts have no column: BCEA s35(4) "remuneration is variable" and s6(3) "above the earnings threshold" | Before the first commission earner, or anybody near R269 600 a year, is paid | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-293). Both calculators take them as declared booleans (D-220, D-110) and nothing captures either: not sheet 02, not the models. The assembly passes FALSE for both - leave pay at the contractual rate, and every premium paid. For the two target sectors at minimum-wage levels that is right for almost everybody and errs towards the employee where it is not; for a variable earner it can UNDER-pay leave (D-221: the average may be higher). The fix is two columns - `employee_remuneration.remuneration_is_variable` and `employee_tax_profile.above_bcea_earnings_threshold`, or wherever Kobus prefers - and a screen to declare them. |
| O-47 | Payroll / Legal | The UIF ceiling on a WEEKLY or FORTNIGHTLY run | Before a weekly-paid employee earns above about R4 087 a week | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-293). UICA s6(2) and the loaded row state the ceiling per MONTH (R17 712); nothing loaded states it per week. Below R17 712 × 12 ÷ 52 in a week (R4 087,38), or twice that in a fortnight, the ceiling cannot bite whichever reading is right, so the assembly hands the monthly figure in unchanged; above it the employee REFUSES (`not_priced:uif_ceiling_not_monthly`) rather than capping on an invented weekly figure. SARS's UIF guide states the annual figure (R212 544); whether the weekly cap is that over 52, or a monthly accumulation, is the question. Domestic workers paid weekly are far below it. |
| O-48 | Security / Tenancy | How sign-in finds the tenants a user belongs to | Before P1's registration, and before tens of tenants | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-296). `tenant_membership` is under FORCE RLS and a request may never enter `platform_context()`, so `core/signin.py::memberships_of()` pins each tenant in turn and asks - correct, and one query per tenant. Fine for a pilot; not for a thousand tenants. The options are a policy clause letting a session see its own user's memberships across tenants (a narrow change to one table's policy, which must move with its manager in the same commit), or a membership index outside the tenant tables. A decision about the RLS design, so Kobus's, not the screen's. |
| O-49 | Payroll / Legal | A fixed recurring amount in a PART period | Before the first mid-month starter or leaver with a recurring line | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-304). A recurring line is priced whole when it is in force on the period's last day. A R600 transport allowance for somebody who started on the 16th is therefore paid in full, and an advance instalment is taken in full from a half month's pay. Neither the BCEA nor the catalogue says whether a fixed allowance follows the days employed; it is often a contract term. The options are a per-component flag (pro-rate on the D-293 working-day fraction, or not) or an employer setting. Not guessed. |
| O-50 | Payroll / Tax | Severance needs a SARS directive, and nothing captures one | Before the first retrenchment is paid | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-308). s41 severance is priced on the termination payout (3901) and the final payslip REFUSES it: a severance benefit is taxed against the retirement/severance lump sum table on a directive SARS issues (IRP3(a)), and `employee_tax_profile`'s directive columns are the employee's ordinary-pay directive, not a lump sum one. Needs: a place to capture the lump sum directive (number, tax amount) and the assembly to deduct exactly that. |
| O-51 | Payroll / Tax | Is payment in lieu of notice an annual payment for PAYE? | Before the first mid-month leaver paid notice in lieu | Raised 24 Sep 2026 (D-306). `NOTICE_PAY` is 3601 in the catalogue, so it is annualised with the period's pay - on a leaver who worked 12 days of June, four weeks' notice lifts the annual equivalent from R108 000 to R324 000 and PAYE with it. SARS's code guide lists leave pay and bonuses under 3605 and does not name notice pay there; the answer changes both the IRP5 code and the tax. Not guessed: the component's code is the catalogue's. |
